# George W. Bush

This article is about the 43rd president of the United States. For his father, the 41st president, see [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush).

**George Walker Bush**[^cite_note-1] (born July 6, 1946) is an American former politician, businessman, and [Air Force](32090.md) veteran who served as the 43rd [president of the United States](24113.md) from 2001 to 2009. A member of the [Republican Party](32070.md), he served as the 46th [governor of Texas](214883.md) from 1995 to 2000. He is the eldest son of [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), the 41st president of the United States.

Born into the prominent [Bush family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_family) in [New Haven, Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven,_Connecticut), Bush flew warplanes in the [Texas Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Air_National_Guard) in his twenties. After graduating from [Harvard Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_Business_School) in 1975, he worked in the oil industry. He later co-owned the [Major League Baseball](38776.md) team [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) before being elected governor of Texas [in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Texas_gubernatorial_election). [As governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorship_of_George_W._Bush), Bush successfully sponsored legislation for [tort reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tort_reform), increased education funding, set higher standards for schools, and reformed the criminal justice system. He also helped make Texas the [leading producer of wind-generated electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Texas) in the United States. In the [2000 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election), he defeated Democratic [incumbent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incumbent) vice president [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) while [losing the popular vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_in_which_the_winner_lost_the_popular_vote) after a narrow and contested [United States Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) win, which involved the Supreme Court decision *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)* to stop [a recount in Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election_recount_in_Florida).

In his first term, Bush signed [a major tax-cut program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_tax_cuts) and an education-reform bill, the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act). He pushed for socially conservative efforts such as the [Partial-Birth Abortion Ban Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partial-Birth_Abortion_Ban_Act), restrictions on same-sex marriage, and [faith-based initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith-based_initiatives). He also initiated the [President's Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Emergency_Plan_for_AIDS_Relief), in 2003, to address the global [AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_epidemic). The [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) during 2001 decisively reshaped his administration, resulting in the start of the [war on terror](13425800.md) and the creation of the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md). Bush ordered the [invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan) in an effort to overthrow the [Taliban](30635.md), destroy [al-Qaeda](1921.md), and capture [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden). He signed the [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act), expanding surveillance powers for suspected terrorists. He also ordered the [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md) to overthrow [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s regime, citing claims that it [possessed weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) and had [ties with al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein_and_al-Qaeda_link_allegations). Bush later signed the [Medicare Modernization Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Prescription_Drug,_Improvement,_and_Modernization_Act), which created [Medicare Part D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Part_D). In 2004, Bush [was re-elected president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election) in a close race, beating Democratic opponent [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) and winning the popular vote.

During his second term, Bush negotiated various [free trade agreements](2533562.md), appointed [John Roberts](1928850.md) and [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito) to the Supreme Court, and sought major changes to [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)) and immigration laws, both of which ultimately failed in Congress. He was widely criticized for [his administration's handling of Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_government_response_to_Hurricane_Katrina) and revelations of [torture against detainees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_torture_and_prisoner_abuse) at [Abu Ghraib prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_prison) in Iraq. Amid his unpopularity, the Democrats regained control of Congress in the [2006 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_United_States_elections). In January 2007, Bush launched [a surge of troops in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge). As his presidency came to a close, the country experienced [a financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), resulting in the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), prompting his administration and Congress to push through emergency economic measures aimed at stabilizing the financial system, including the [Troubled Asset Relief Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troubled_Asset_Relief_Program).

After his second term, Bush returned to Texas, where he has maintained a low public profile. At various points in his presidency, he was both among the most popular and among the least popular presidents in U.S. history. He received the highest recorded approval ratings in the wake of the September 11 attacks, and one of the lowest ratings during the 2008 financial crisis. Bush left office as one of the most unpopular U.S. presidents; however, [public opinion of him](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_George_W._Bush) has improved in [his post-presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-presidency_of_George_W._Bush). [Scholars and historians generally rank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States) Bush as a below-average president.

## Early life and education

Main article: [Early life of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_life_of_George_W._Bush)



Bush with his parents, [Barbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Bush) and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in 1947

George Walker Bush was born on July 6, 1946, at [Grace-New Haven Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace-New_Haven_Hospital) in [New Haven, Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Haven,_Connecticut).[^cite_note-2] He is the first child of [George Herbert Walker Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Herbert_Walker_Bush) and [Barbara Pierce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Pierce), and was raised in [Midland](151222.md) and [Houston](13774.md), Texas. His five siblings are [Robin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_Robinson_Bush), [Jeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeb_Bush), [Neil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Bush), [Marvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvin_Bush) and [Dorothy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothy_Bush_Koch). Robin died from [leukemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leukemia) at the age of three in 1953.[^cite_note-3] His paternal grandfather, [Prescott Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescott_Bush), was a U.S. senator from [Connecticut](6466.md).[^cite_note-4] His father was [Ronald Reagan](25433.md)'s vice president from 1981 to 1989 and the 41st president of the United States from 1989 to 1993. Bush has distant [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans) and [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) ancestry, along with more distant (over seven generations removed) [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Americans), [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Americans), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Americans), and [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Americans) roots.[^cite_note-5]

Bush attended public schools in Midland, Texas, until the family moved to Houston after he had completed seventh grade. He then spent two years at [The Kinkaid School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kinkaid_School), a [college-preparatory school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College-preparatory_school) in [Piney Point Village, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piney_Point_Village,_Texas).[^cite_note-6]

Bush later attended [Phillips Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phillips_Academy), a boarding school in [Andover, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andover,_Massachusetts), where he played baseball and was the head cheerleader during his senior year.[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] He attended [Yale University](34273.md) from 1964 to 1968, graduating with a [Bachelor of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts) degree in history.[^cite_note-whitehousebio-9] During this time, he was a cheerleader and a member of the [Delta Kappa Epsilon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Kappa_Epsilon), serving as the president of the fraternity during his senior year.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] Bush became [a member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Skull_and_Bones_members) of the [Skull and Bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull_and_Bones) society as a senior.[^cite_note-13] Bush was a [rugby union](25405.md) player and was on Yale's 1st XV.[^cite_note-14] He characterized himself as an average student.[^cite_note-15] His [grade point average](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_point_average) during his first three years at Yale was 77, and he had a similar average under a nonnumerical rating system in his final year.[^cite_note-16]

In the fall of 1973, Bush entered [Harvard Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_Business_School). He graduated in 1975 with a [Master of Business Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_of_Business_Administration). He is the only U.S. president to have earned that degree.[^cite_note-17]

## Career

### Military career

Main article: [George W. Bush military service controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_military_service_controversy)

See also: [Killian documents controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killian_documents_controversy) and [Killian documents authenticity issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killian_documents_authenticity_issues)

In May 1968, Bush joined the [United States Air Force](32090.md) and was commissioned into the [Texas Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Air_National_Guard).[^cite_note-wpbushguardquestion-18] After two years of training in active-duty service,[^cite_note-lardner19990728-19] he was assigned to [Houston](13774.md), flying [Convair F-102s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convair_F-102) with the [147th Reconnaissance Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/147th_Reconnaissance_Wing) out of [Ellington Field Joint Reserve Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellington_Field_Joint_Reserve_Base).[^cite_note-wpbushguardquestion-18][^cite_note-20] Critics, including former [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee) Chairman [Terry McAuliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_McAuliffe), have alleged that Bush was favorably treated due to his father's political standing as a member of [the House of Representatives](19468510.md), citing his selection as a pilot despite his low pilot aptitude test scores and his irregular attendance.[^cite_note-wpbushguardquestion-18] In June 2005, [the Department of Defense](7279897.md) released all the records of Bush's Texas Air National Guard service, which remain in its official archives.[^cite_note-USDoDbushrecords-21]

In late 1972 and early 1973, he drilled with the [187th Fighter Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/187th_Fighter_Wing) of the [Alabama Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Air_National_Guard). He had moved to [Montgomery, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama), to work on the unsuccessful U.S. Senate campaign of Republican [Winton M. Blount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winton_M._Blount).[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] In 1972, Bush was suspended from flying for failure to take a scheduled physical exam.[^cite_note-24] He was honorably discharged from the [Air Force Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Reserve) on November 21, 1974.[^cite_note-25]

Bush remains the most recent president to have served in the military.[^cite_note-26]

### Business career

Main article: [Professional life of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_life_of_George_W._Bush)



Bush with former president [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) in August 1984

In 1977, Bush established [Arbusto Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbusto_Energy), a small [oil exploration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_exploration) company, which began operations in 1978.[^cite_note-Lardner-27][^cite_note-28] He later changed the name to Bush Exploration. In 1984, his company merged with the larger [Spectrum 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectrum_7), and Bush became chairman. The company was hurt by decreased oil prices, and it folded into [Harken Energy Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harken_Energy_Corporation),[^cite_note-29] with Bush becoming a member of Harken's board of directors. Questions of [possible insider trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_insider_trading_allegations) involving Harken arose, but a [Securities and Exchange Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Securities_and_Exchange_Commission) investigation concluded that the information Bush had at the time of his stock sale was not sufficient to constitute insider trading.[^cite_note-30]

In April 1989, Bush arranged for a group of investors to purchase a controlling interest in Major League Baseball's [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) for \$89 million and invested \$500,000 himself to start. He was then the managing general partner for five years.[^cite_note-TexRngrs-31] He actively led the team's projects and regularly attended its games, often choosing to sit in the open stands with fans.[^cite_note-32] Bush's sale of his shares in the Rangers in 1998 brought him over \$15 million from his initial \$800,000 investment.[^cite_note-33]

In the early or mid 1990s, before his gubernatorial campaign, Bush briefly considered running for [Commissioner of Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner_of_Baseball).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36]

### Early political involvement

[In 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), Bush ran for the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) from [Texas's 19th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_19th_congressional_district). The retiring member, [George H. Mahon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._Mahon), had held the district for the Democratic Party since 1935. Bush's opponent, [Kent Hance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_Hance), portrayed him as out of touch with rural Texans, and Bush lost the election, receiving 46.8 percent of the vote to Hance's 53.2 percent.[^cite_note-NewsMine-37]

Bush and his family moved to Washington, D.C., in 1988 to work on [his father's campaign for the U.S. presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush_1988_presidential_campaign).[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] He was a campaign advisor and liaison to the media, and assisted his father by campaigning across the country.[^cite_note-msn-40] In December 1991, Bush was one of seven people named by his father to run his father's 1992 presidential [re-election campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush_1992_presidential_campaign) as a campaign advisor.[^cite_note-41] The previous month, his father had asked him to tell White House chief of staff [John H. Sununu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_H._Sununu) to resign.[^cite_note-42]

## Governor of Texas (1995–2000)

Main article: [Governorship of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorship_of_George_W._Bush)



Bush during his governorship, 1999

Bush declared his candidacy for the [1994 Texas gubernatorial election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Texas_gubernatorial_election) at the same time his brother Jeb [sought the governorship in Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Florida_gubernatorial_election). His campaign focused on four themes: welfare reform, [tort reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tort_reform), crime reduction, and education improvement.[^cite_note-msn-40] Bush's campaign advisers were [Karen Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Hughes), [Joe Allbaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Allbaugh), and [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove).[^cite_note-SlaterBrain-43]

After easily winning the Republican primary, Bush faced popular Democratic incumbent governor [Ann Richards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Richards).[^cite_note-msn-40][^cite_note-telegraph-44] In the course of the campaign, Bush pledged to sign a bill allowing Texans to obtain [permits to carry concealed weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concealed_carry_permit). Richards had vetoed the bill, but Bush signed it into law after he became governor.[^cite_note-concealed_carry-45] According to *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)*, the race "featured a rumor that she was a lesbian, along with a rare instance of such a tactic's making it into the public record – when a regional chairman of the Bush campaign allowed himself, perhaps inadvertently, to be quoted criticizing Richards for 'appointing avowed [homosexual activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_social_movements)' to state jobs".[^cite_note-46] *The Atlantic*, and others, connected the lesbian rumor to Karl Rove,[^cite_note-47] but Rove denied being involved.[^cite_note-48] Bush won the general election with 53.5 percent of the vote against Richards' 45.9 percent.[^cite_note-49]

Bush used a budget surplus to push through Texas's largest [tax cut](455990.md), \$2 billion.[^cite_note-SlaterBrain-43] He extended government funding for organizations providing education on the dangers of alcohol and [drug use and abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_abuse), and helping to reduce [domestic violence](18978563.md).[^cite_note-50] His administration lowered the age at which juveniles can be sent to adult court for serious crimes to 14.[^cite_note-51] Bush presided over 152 executions,[^cite_note-52] more than any previous governor in modern American history; critics such as [Helen Prejean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Prejean) argue that he failed to give serious consideration to [clemency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemency) requests.[^cite_note-executions-53][^cite_note-54] Critics also contended that during his tenure, Texas ranked near the bottom in environmental evaluations. Supporters pointed to his efforts to raise the salaries of teachers and improve educational test scores.[^cite_note-msn-40]

In 1999, Bush signed [a law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_portfolio_standard) that required electric retailers to buy a certain amount of energy from renewable sources (RPS),[^cite_note-txlaw-55][^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] which helped [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Texas) eventually become the leading producer of [wind-powered electricity in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_the_United_States)[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-mighty_wind-59]

[In 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Texas_gubernatorial_election), Bush won re-election with a record[^cite_note-msn-40] 68 percent of the vote.[^cite_note-60] He became the first governor in Texas history to be elected to two consecutive four-year terms.[^cite_note-msn-40] During his second term, Bush promoted faith-based organizations and enjoyed high [approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Approval_rating), which ranged between 62 and 81 percent.[^cite_note-msn-40][^cite_note-61] He proclaimed June 10, 2000, to be [Jesus Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Day) in Texas, a day on which he urged all Texans to "answer the call to serve those in need".[^cite_note-62]

Throughout Bush's first term, he was the focus of national attention as a potential future presidential candidate. Following his re-election, speculation soared, and within a year he decided to seek the 2000 Republican presidential nomination.[^cite_note-msn-40]

## Presidential campaigns

### 2000 presidential campaign

Main articles: [George W. Bush 2000 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_2000_presidential_campaign), [2000 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), and [2000 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election)

#### Primary

Bush portrayed himself as a [compassionate conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compassionate_conservative), implying he was more centrist than other Republicans. He campaigned on a platform which included bringing integrity and honor back to the White House, increasing the size of the military, cutting taxes, improving education, and aiding minorities.[^cite_note-msn-40] By early 2000, the race had centered on Bush and Arizona senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain).[^cite_note-msn-40]

Bush won the [Iowa caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa_caucuses) and, although heavily favored to win the [New Hampshire primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hampshire_primary), trailed McCain by 19% and lost. Despite this, he regained momentum and effectively became the front runner after the [South Carolina primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_presidential_primaries,_2000), which according to *[The Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe)* made history for his campaign's negativity. *[The New York Times](30680.md)* described it as a [smear campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smear_campaign).[^cite_note-anatomy-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65]

#### General election

On July 25, 2000, Bush surprised some observers when he selected [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) – a former [White House chief of staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_chief_of_staff), U.S. representative, and secretary of defense – to be his running mate. At the time, Cheney was serving as head of Bush's vice presidential search committee. Soon after at the [2000 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Republican_National_Convention), Bush and Cheney were officially nominated by the Republican Party.[^cite_note-66]

Bush continued to campaign across the country and touted his record as Governor of Texas.[^cite_note-msn-40] During his campaign, Bush criticized his Democratic opponent, incumbent vice president [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), over [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_law_in_the_United_States) and taxation.[^cite_note-67]

When the election returns were tallied on November 7, Bush had won 29 states, including Florida. The closeness of the Florida outcome led to [a recount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election_in_Florida,_2000).[^cite_note-msn-40] The initial recount also went to Bush, but the outcome was tied up in lower courts for a month until eventually reaching the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md).[^cite_note-68] On December 9, in the controversial *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)* ruling,[^cite_note-69] the Court reversed a [Florida Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Supreme_Court) decision that had ordered a third count, and stopped an ordered statewide hand recount based on the argument that the use of different standards among Florida's counties violated the [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause) of the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md).[^cite_note-msn-40] The machine recount showed that Bush had won the Florida vote by a margin of 537 votes out of six million casts.[^cite_note-2000results-70] Although he had received 543,895 fewer individual nationwide votes than Gore, Bush won the election, receiving 271 [electoral votes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_College_\(United_States\)) to Gore's 266 (Gore had actually been awarded 267 votes by the states pledged to him plus the District of Columbia, but one D.C. elector abstained). Bush was the first person to [win a U.S. presidential election with fewer popular votes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_in_which_the_winner_lost_the_popular_vote) than another candidate since [Benjamin Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison) in 1888.[^cite_note-2000results-70]

### 2004 presidential campaign

Main articles: [George W. Bush 2004 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_2004_presidential_campaign), [2004 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), and [2004 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election)

In his 2004 bid for re-election, Bush commanded broad support in the Republican Party and did not encounter a primary challenge. He appointed [Ken Mehlman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Mehlman) as campaign manager, and [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove) devised a political strategy.[^cite_note-71] Bush and the Republican platform emphasized a strong commitment to the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan,[^cite_note-platform04-72] support for the [USA PATRIOT Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_PATRIOT_Act),[^cite_note-73] a renewed shift in policy for constitutional amendments banning abortion and [same-sex marriage](92656.md),[^cite_note-platform04-72][^cite_note-74] reforming [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)) to create private investment accounts,[^cite_note-platform04-72] creation of an [ownership society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ownership_society),[^cite_note-platform04-72] and opposing mandatory carbon emissions controls.[^cite_note-75] Bush also called for the implementation of a [guest worker program](5227594.md) for immigrants,[^cite_note-platform04-72] which was criticized by conservatives.[^cite_note-76]

The Bush campaign advertised across the U.S. against Democratic candidates, including Bush's emerging opponent, Massachusetts senator [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry). Kerry and other Democrats attacked Bush on the [Iraq War](5043324.md), and accused him of failing to stimulate the economy and job growth. The Bush campaign portrayed Kerry as a staunch [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States) who would raise taxes and increase the size of government. The Bush campaign continuously criticized Kerry's seemingly contradictory statements on the war in Iraq,[^cite_note-msn-40] and argued that Kerry lacked the decisiveness and vision necessary for success in the war on terror.

Following the resignation of CIA director [George Tenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Tenet) in 2004, Bush nominated [Porter Goss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porter_Goss) to head the agency. The White House ordered Goss to purge agency officers who were disloyal to the administration.[^cite_note-salon-77] After Goss' appointment, many of the CIA's senior agents were fired or quit. The CIA has been accused of deliberately leaking classified information to undermine the 2004 election.[^cite_note-wapo-78]

In the election, Bush carried 31 of 50 states, receiving 286 electoral votes. He won an absolute majority of the popular vote (50.7% to Kerry's 48.3%).[^cite_note-16_years-79]

## Presidency (2001–2009)

Main article: [Presidency of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the George W. Bush presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_George_W._Bush_presidency).

See also: [List of George W. Bush legislation and programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_George_W._Bush_legislation_and_programs)



Bush takes [the oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_President_of_the_United_States) administered by [Chief Justice](31739.md) [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist) at [the Capitol](31979.md) on January 20, 2001.



Bush takes the oath of office to a second term.

Bush had originally outlined an ambitious domestic agenda, but his priorities were significantly altered following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-80] Wars were begun in Afghanistan and Iraq, and there were significant domestic debates regarding immigration, healthcare, Social Security, economic policy, and treatment of terrorist detainees. Over an eight-year period, Bush's once-high approval ratings[^cite_note-gallup_high-81] steadily declined, while his disapproval numbers increased significantly.[^cite_note-wapo_ratings-82] In 2007, the United States entered the longest post-[World War II](32927.md) recession.[^cite_note-longest1-83]

### Domestic policy

Main article: [Domestic policy of the George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration)

#### Economic policy

Main article: [Economic policy of the George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration)

Bush took office during an economic recession in the wake of the bursting of the [dot-com bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dot-com_bubble).[^cite_note-84] The September 11 terrorist attacks also [impacted the economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_effects_of_the_September_11_attacks).

His administration increased federal [government spending](1203070.md) from \$1.8 trillion to \$3 trillion (66%), while revenues increased from \$2 trillion to \$2.5 trillion (from 2000 to 2008). Individual income tax revenues increased by 14%, corporate tax revenues by 50%, and customs and duties by 40%. Discretionary defense spending was increased by 107%, discretionary domestic spending by 62%, Medicare spending by 131%, social security by 51%, and income security spending by 130%. Cyclically adjusted, revenues rose by 35% and spending by 65%.[^cite_note-85] The increase in spending was more than under any predecessor since [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson).[^cite_note-86] The number of [economic regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_regulation) governmental workers increased by 91,196.[^cite_note-bushregulation-87]

The surplus in 2000 was \$237 billion – the third consecutive surplus and the largest surplus ever.[^cite_note-omb-88] In 2001, Bush's budget estimated that there would be a \$5.6 trillion surplus over the next ten years.[^cite_note-89] Facing congressional opposition, Bush held town hall-style meetings across the U.S. to increase public support for his plan for [a \$1.35 trillion tax cut program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Growth_and_Tax_Relief_Reconciliation_Act_of_2001), one of the largest tax cuts in history.[^cite_note-msn-40] Bush argued that unspent government funds should be returned to taxpayers, saying "the surplus is not the government's money. The surplus is the people's money."[^cite_note-msn-40] Federal Reserve chairman [Alan Greenspan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Greenspan) warned of a recession and Bush stated that a tax cut would stimulate the economy and create jobs.[^cite_note-90] Treasury Secretary [Paul H. O'Neill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_H._O'Neill) opposed some of the tax cuts on the basis they would contribute to budget deficits and undermine [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-91] O'Neill disputes the claim, made in Bush's book *Decision Points*, that he never openly disagreed with him on planned tax cuts.[^cite_note-92] By 2003, the economy showed signs of improvement, though job growth remained stagnant.[^cite_note-msn-40] [Another tax cut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jobs_and_Growth_Tax_Relief_Reconciliation_Act_of_2003) was passed that year.[^cite_note-93]

Between 2001 and 2008, GDP grew at an average annual rate of 2.125%,[^cite_note-94] less than for past business cycles.[^cite_note-Price_&_Ratner-95] Bush entered office with the [Dow Jones Industrial Average](47361.md) at 10,587, and the average peaked in October 2007 at over 14,000. When Bush left office, the average was at 7,949, one of the lowest levels of his presidency.[^cite_note-96] Only four other U.S. presidents have left office with the stock market lower than when they began.[^cite_note-97]

Unemployment originally rose from 4.2% in January 2001 to 6.3% in June 2003, but subsequently dropped to 4.5% in July 2007.[^cite_note-98] Adjusted for inflation, [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_household_income) dropped by \$1,175 between 2000 and 2007,[^cite_note-99] while Professor Ken Homa of [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University) noted that "Median real after-tax household income went up two percent".[^cite_note-100] The poverty rate increased from 11.3% in 2000 to 12.3% in 2006 after peaking at 12.7% in 2004.[^cite_note-101] By October 2008, due to increases in spending,[^cite_note-greenburg-102]<sup>: 273 </sup> the [U.S. national debt](322221.md) had risen to \$11.3 trillion,[^cite_note-103] more than doubling it since 2000.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105] Most debt was accumulated as a result of what became known as the "[Bush tax cuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_tax_cuts)" and increased national security spending.[^cite_note-106] In March 2006, then-senator [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) said when he voted against raising the [debt ceiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_debt_ceiling): "The fact that we are here today to debate raising America's debt limit is a sign of leadership failure."[^cite_note-107] By the end of Bush's presidency, unemployment climbed to 7.2%.[^cite_note-108]

#### 2008 financial crisis

In December 2007, the United States entered the longest post–[World War II](32927.md) recession,[^cite_note-longest1-83] [caused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causes_of_the_Great_Recession) by a [housing market correction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_housing_market_correction), a [subprime mortgage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subprime_mortgage_crisis), [soaring oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_energy_crisis), and other factors. In February 2008, 63,000 jobs were lost, a five-year record,[^cite_note-109] and in November, over 500,000 jobs were lost, which marked the largest loss of jobs in the United States in 34 years.[^cite_note-110] The [Bureau of Labor Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics) reported that in the last four months of 2008, 1.9 million jobs were lost.[^cite_note-111] By the end of 2008, the U.S. had lost 2.6 million jobs.[^cite_note-112]

To aid with the situation, Bush signed a \$170 billion economic stimulus package which was intended to improve the economic situation by sending tax rebate checks to many Americans and providing tax breaks for struggling businesses.[^cite_note-113] The Bush administration pushed for significantly increased regulation of [Fannie Mae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Mae) and [Freddie Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddie_Mac) in 2003,[^cite_note-114] and after two years, the regulations passed the House but died in the Senate. Many Republican senators, as well as influential members of the Bush Administration, feared that the agency created by these regulations would merely be mimicking the private sector's risky practices.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-admin_crisis-116][^cite_note-117] In September 2008, the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) intensified, beginning with the [Federal takeover of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_takeover_of_Fannie_Mae_and_Freddie_Mac) followed by the [bankruptcy of Lehman Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankruptcy_of_Lehman_Brothers) and a federal bailout of [American International Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_International_Group) for \$85 billion.[^cite_note-118]

Many economists and world governments determined that the situation had become the worst financial crisis since the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] Additional regulation over the housing market would have been beneficial, according to former Federal Reserve chairman [Alan Greenspan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Greenspan).[^cite_note-121] Bush, meanwhile, proposed [a financial rescue plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Economic_Stabilization_Act_of_2008) to buy back a large portion of the U.S. mortgage market.[^cite_note-122] Vince Reinhardt, a former Federal Reserve economist now at the [American Enterprise Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Enterprise_Institute), said "it would have helped for the Bush administration to empower the folks at Treasury and the Federal Reserve and the comptroller of the currency and the [FDIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FDIC) to look at these issues more closely", and additionally, that it would have helped "for Congress to have held hearings".[^cite_note-admin_crisis-116]

#### Education and public health



President Bush signing the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act) into law on January 8, 2002

Bush pursued many educational initiatives, such as increasing the funding for the [National Science Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Science_Foundation) and [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) in his first years of office and creating education programs to strengthen the grounding in science and mathematics for American high school students. Funding for the NIH was cut in 2006, the first such cut in 36 years, due to rising inflation.[^cite_note-123]

One of the administration's early major initiatives was the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act), which aimed to measure and close the gap between rich and poor student performance, provide options to parents with students in low-performing schools, and target more federal funding to low-income schools. This landmark education initiative passed with broad bipartisan support, including the support of Senator [Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy) of Massachusetts.[^cite_note-124] It was signed into law by Bush in early 2002.[^cite_note-125] Many contend that the initiative has been successful, as cited by the fact that students in the U.S. have performed significantly better on state reading and math tests since Bush signed "No Child Left Behind" into law.[^cite_note-126] Critics argue that it is underfunded[^cite_note-127] and that NCLBA's focus on "high-stakes testing" and quantitative outcomes is counterproductive.[^cite_note-128]

On November 1, 2005, Bush launched a *National Strategy for [Pandemic Influenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flu_pandemic)*, which culminated in an implementation plan published by the Homeland Security Council in May 2006.[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130]

After being re-elected, Bush signed into law a [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) drug benefit program which, according to [Jan Crawford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Crawford), resulted in "the greatest expansion in America's [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) in forty years" – the bill's costs approached \$7 trillion.[^cite_note-greenburg-102]<sup>: 274 </sup> In 2007, Bush opposed and vetoed [State Children's Health Insurance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Children's_Health_Insurance_Program) (SCHIP) legislation, which was added by the Democrats onto a war funding bill and passed by Congress. The SCHIP legislation would have significantly expanded federally funded healthcare benefits and plans to children of some low-income families. It was to be funded by an increase in the cigarette tax.[^cite_note-131] Bush viewed the legislation as a move toward [socialized health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialized_health_care), and asserted that the program could benefit families making as much as \$83,000 per year who did not need the help.[^cite_note-132]

On May 21, 2008, Bush signed into law the [Genetic Information Nondiscrimination Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_Information_Nondiscrimination_Act), aimed to protect Americans against health insurance and employment discrimination based on a person's genetic information. The issue had been debated for 13 years before it finally became law. The measure is designed to protect citizens without hindering genetic research.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134]

#### Social services and Social Security

Following Republican efforts to pass the [Medicare Act of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Prescription_Drug,_Improvement,_and_Modernization_Act), Bush signed the bill, which included major changes to the [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) program by providing beneficiaries with some assistance in paying for prescription drugs, while relying on private insurance for the delivery of benefits.[^cite_note-135] The retired persons lobby group [AARP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AARP) worked with the Bush Administration on the program and gave its endorsement. Bush said the law, estimated to cost \$400 billion over the first ten years, would give the elderly "better choices and more control over their health care".[^cite_note-136]

Bush began his second term by outlining a major initiative to [reform Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_debate_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-ss-msnbc-137] which was facing record deficit projections beginning in 2005. Bush made it the centerpiece of his domestic agenda despite opposition from some in the U.S. Congress.[^cite_note-ss-msnbc-137] In his [2005 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush discussed the potential impending bankruptcy of the program and outlined his new program, which included partial privatization of the system, personal Social Security accounts, and options to permit Americans to divert a portion of their Social Security tax ([FICA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FICA)) into secured investments.[^cite_note-ss-msnbc-137] Democrats opposed the proposal to partially privatize the system.[^cite_note-ss-msnbc-137]

Bush embarked on a 60-day national tour, campaigning for his initiative in media events known as "Conversations on Social Security" in an attempt to gain public support.[^cite_note-138] Nevertheless, public support for the proposal declined,[^cite_note-139] and the House Republican leadership decided not to put Social Security reform on the priority list for the remainder of their 2005 legislative agenda.[^cite_note-140] The proposal's legislative prospects were further diminished by autumn 2005 due to political fallout from the response to [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina).[^cite_note-141]

#### Environmental policies

Main article: [Domestic policy of the George W. Bush administration § Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration#Environment)

Upon taking office in 2001, Bush stated his opposition to the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol), an amendment to the [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md) which seeks to impose mandatory targets for reducing [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md), citing that the treaty exempted 80% of the world's population[^cite_note-142] and would have cost tens of billions of dollars per year.[^cite_note-143] He also cited that the Senate had voted 95–0 in 1997 on a resolution expressing its disapproval of the protocol.

In May 2001, Bush signed an [executive order](210541.md) to create an interagency task force to streamline energy projects,[^cite_note-eo13212-144] and later signed two other executive orders to tackle environmental issues.[^cite_note-sovaWater-145]

In 2002, Bush proposed the [Clear Skies Act of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clear_Skies_Act_of_2003),[^cite_note-EXsummary-146] which aimed at amending the [Clean Air Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_Air_Act_\(United_States\)) to reduce air pollution through the use of [emissions trading](37104.md) programs. Many experts argued that this legislation would have weakened the original legislation by allowing higher emission rates of pollutants than were previously legal.[^cite_note-147] The initiative was introduced to Congress, but failed to make it out of committee.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149]

Later in 2006, Bush declared the [Northwestern Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Hawaiian_Islands) a national monument, creating the largest [marine reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_reserve) to date. The [Papahānaumokuākea Marine National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papah%C4%81naumoku%C4%81kea_Marine_National_Monument) comprises 84 million acres (340,000 km<sup>2</sup>) and is home to 7,000 species of fish, birds, and other marine animals, many of which are specific to only those islands.[^cite_note-150] The move was hailed by conservationists for "its foresight and leadership in protecting this incredible area".[^cite_note-151]

Bush has said he believes that [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming) is real[^cite_note-152] and has noted that it is a serious problem, but he asserted there is a "debate over whether it's man-made or naturally caused".[^cite_note-153] The Bush Administration's stance on global warming remained controversial in the scientific and environmental communities. Critics have alleged that the administration[^cite_note-154] misinformed the public and did not do enough to reduce [carbon emissions](21350772.md#Greenhouse_gas_emissions_from_human_activities) and deter global warming.[^cite_note-155]

#### Energy policies

In his [2006 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush declared, "America is addicted to oil" and launched his *Advanced Energy Initiative* to increase [energy development](533487.md) research.[^cite_note-156]

In his [2007 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush renewed his pledge to work toward diminished reliance on foreign oil by reducing [fossil fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel) consumption and increasing [alternative fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_fuel) production.[^cite_note-157] Amid high gasoline prices in 2008, Bush lifted a ban on [offshore drilling](9969688.md).[^cite_note-drilling-cnn-158] However, the move was largely symbolic because there was still a federal law banning offshore drilling. Bush said: "This means that the only thing standing between the American people and these vast oil reserves is action from the U.S. Congress."[^cite_note-drilling-cnn-158] Bush had said in June 2008, "In the long run, the solution is to reduce demand for oil by promoting alternative energy technologies. My administration has worked with Congress to invest in gas-saving technologies like advanced batteries and hydrogen [fuel cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel_cell) ... In the short run, the American economy will continue to rely largely on oil. And that means we need to increase supply, especially here at home. So my administration has repeatedly called on Congress to expand domestic oil production."[^cite_note-159]

In his [2008 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush committed \$2 billion over the next three years to a new international fund to promote clean energy technologies and fight climate change, saying, "Along with contributions from other countries, this fund will increase and accelerate the deployment of all forms of cleaner, more efficient technologies in developing nations like India and China, and help leverage substantial private-sector capital by making clean energy projects more financially attractive." He also presented plans to reaffirm the United States' commitment to work with major economies, and, through the UN, to complete an international agreement which will slow, stop, and eventually reverse the growth of greenhouse gases; he stated, "This agreement will be effective only if it includes commitments by every major economy and gives none a free ride."[^cite_note-160]

#### Stem cell research and first veto

Federal funding for medical research involving the creation or destruction of human embryos through the [Department of Health and Human Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services) and the [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) has been forbidden by law since the passage of the [Dickey–Wicker Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dickey%E2%80%93Wicker_Amendment) in 1995.[^cite_note-161] Bush has said he supports adult [stem cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_cell) research and has supported federal legislation that finances adult stem cell research. However, Bush did not support [embryonic stem cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_stem_cell) research.[^cite_note-162] On August 9, 2001, Bush signed an executive order lifting the ban on federal funding for the 71 existing "lines" of stem cells,[^cite_note-163] but the ability of these existing lines to provide an adequate medium for testing has been questioned. Testing can be done on only 12 of the original lines, and all approved lines have been cultured in contact with mouse cells, creating safety issues that complicate development and approval of therapies from these lines.[^cite_note-164] On July 19, 2006, Bush used his veto power for the first time in his presidency to veto the [Stem Cell Research Enhancement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_Cell_Research_Enhancement_Act). The bill would have repealed the Dickey–Wicker Amendment, thereby permitting federal money to be used for research where stem cells are derived from the destruction of an embryo.[^cite_note-165]

#### Immigration

Nearly eight million immigrants came to the U.S. from 2000 to 2005, more than in any other five-year period in the nation's history.[^cite_note-166] Almost half entered illegally.[^cite_note-167] In 2006, Bush urged Congress to allow more than twelve million [illegal immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigrants) to work in the United States with the creation of a "temporary guest-worker program". Bush also urged Congress to provide additional funds for border security and committed to deploying 6,000 [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_of_the_United_States) troops to the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border).[^cite_note-168] From May to June 2007, Bush strongly supported the [Comprehensive Immigration Reform Act of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Immigration_Reform_Act_of_2007), which was written by a bipartisan group of senators with the active participation of the Bush administration.[^cite_note-169] The bill envisioned a legalization program for illegal immigrants, with an eventual path to citizenship; the establishment of a guest worker program; a series of border and worksite enforcement measures; a reform of the [green card](300237.md) application process and the introduction of a point-based "merit" system for green cards; elimination of "[chain migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_migration)" and of the [Diversity Immigrant Visa](490086.md); and other measures. Bush argued that the lack of legal status denies the protections of U.S. laws to millions of people who face dangers of poverty and exploitation, and penalizes employers despite a demand for immigrant labor.[^cite_note-170] Bush contended that the proposed bill did not amount to amnesty.[^cite_note-171]

A heated public debate followed, which resulted in a substantial rift within the Republican Party; most conservatives opposed it because of its legalization or amnesty provisions.[^cite_note-172] The bill was eventually defeated in the Senate on June 28, 2007, when a [cloture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture) motion failed on a 46–53 vote.[^cite_note-173] Bush expressed disappointment upon the defeat of one of his signature domestic initiatives.[^cite_note-174] The Bush administration later proposed a series of immigration enforcement measures that do not require a change in law.[^cite_note-175]

On September 19, 2010, former Israeli Prime Minister [Ehud Olmert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Olmert) said that Bush offered to accept 100,000 Palestinian refugees as American citizens if a permanent settlement had been reached between Israel and the Palestinian Authority.[^cite_note-176]

#### Hurricane Katrina

Main article: [Political effects of Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_effects_of_Hurricane_Katrina)



President Bush with hurricane victims in [Biloxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biloxi) on September 2, 2005

[Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) struck early in Bush's second term and was one of the most damaging natural disasters in U.S. history. Katrina formed in late August during the [2005 Atlantic hurricane season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Atlantic_hurricane_season) and devastated much of the north-central [Gulf Coast of the United States](11969.md), particularly New Orleans.[^cite_note-177]

Bush declared a state of emergency in [Louisiana](18130.md) on August 27[^cite_note-178] and in [Mississippi](16949861.md) and [Alabama](303.md) the following day.[^cite_note-179] The eye of the hurricane made landfall on August 29, and New Orleans began to flood due to [levee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levee) breaches; later that day, Bush declared a major disaster in Louisiana,[^cite_note-180] officially authorizing FEMA to start using federal funds to assist in the recovery effort.

On August 30, DHS Secretary [Michael Chertoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Chertoff) declared it "an incident of national significance",[^cite_note-181] triggering the first use of the newly created [National Response Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Response_Plan). Three days later, on September 2, National Guard troops first entered the city of New Orleans.[^cite_note-tpm-182] The same day, Bush toured parts of Louisiana, Mississippi, and Alabama and declared that the success of the recovery effort up to that point was "not enough".[^cite_note-USAToday-Katrina-183]

As the disaster in New Orleans intensified, Bush received [widespread criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_government_response_to_Hurricane_Katrina) for downplaying his administration's role in the inadequate response. Leaders attacked Bush for having appointed incompetent leaders to positions of power at FEMA, notably [Michael D. Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_D._Brown);[^cite_note-184] federal resources to respond were also limited as a result of being allocated to the [Iraq War](5043324.md),[^cite_note-185] and Bush himself did not act upon warnings of floods.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] Bush responded to mounting criticism by claiming to accept full responsibility for the federal government's failures in its handling of the emergency.[^cite_note-tpm-182] It has been argued that with Katrina, Bush passed a political tipping point from which he would not recover.[^cite_note-188]

#### Midterm dismissal of U.S. attorneys

Main article: [2006 dismissal of U.S. attorneys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_dismissal_of_U.S._attorneys)

During Bush's second term, a controversy arose over the [Justice Department's](52563.md) midterm dismissal of seven [United States Attorneys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Attorney).[^cite_note-189] The White House maintained that they were fired for poor performance.[^cite_note-190] Attorney General [Alberto Gonzales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Gonzales) later resigned over the issue, along with other senior members of the Justice Department.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] The [House Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Judiciary_Committee) issued subpoenas for advisers [Harriet Miers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Miers) and [Josh Bolten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Bolten) to testify regarding this matter, but Bush directed Miers and Bolten not to comply with those subpoenas, invoking his right of [executive privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_privilege). Bush maintained that all his advisers were covered under a broad executive privilege protection to receive candid advice. The Justice Department determined that the President's order was legal.[^cite_note-193]

Although congressional investigations focused on whether the Justice Department and the White House were using the U.S. Attorney positions for political advantage, no official findings have been released. On March 10, 2008, the Congress filed a federal lawsuit to enforce their issued subpoenas.[^cite_note-194] On July 31, 2008, a [United States district court](70235.md) judge ruled that Bush's top advisers were not immune from congressional subpoenas.[^cite_note-195]

In all, twelve Justice Department officials resigned rather than testify under oath before Congress. They included Attorney General [Alberto Gonzales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Gonzales)[^cite_note-196] and his chief of staff [Kyle Sampson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Sampson),[^cite_note-197] Gonzales' liaison to the White House [Monica Goodling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monica_Goodling),[^cite_note-198] aide to the president [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove)[^cite_note-199] and his senior aide [Sara Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_Taylor).[^cite_note-washingtonpost2007-200] In addition, legal counsel to the president [Harriet Miers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Miers)[^cite_note-twssffwe-201] and deputy chief of staff to the president [Joshua Bolten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Bolten)[^cite_note-twssffwe-201] were both found in [contempt of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contempt_of_Congress).[^cite_note-washingtonpost2007-200]

In 2010, the Justice Department investigator concluded that though political considerations did play a part in as many as four of the attorney firings,[^cite_note-202] the firings were "inappropriately political" but not criminal. According to the prosecutors, there was insufficient evidence to pursue prosecution for any criminal offense.[^cite_note-203]

### Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign policy of the George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration)

During his presidential campaign, Bush's [foreign policy](7564.md) platform included support for stronger economic and political relationships with Latin America, especially Mexico, and a reduction of involvement in "[nation-building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation-building)" and other small-scale military engagements. The administration pursued a [national missile defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_missile_defense).[^cite_note-204] Bush was an advocate of China's entry into the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization).[^cite_note-205]

Bush began his second term with an emphasis on improving strained relations with European nations. He appointed long-time adviser [Karen Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Hughes) to oversee a global public relations campaign. Bush lauded the pro-democracy struggles in Georgia and Ukraine.[^cite_note-206]

In March 2006, Bush visited India on a trip focused particularly on areas of [nuclear energy](22153.md), counter-terrorism co-operation, and discussions that would eventually lead to the [India–United States Civil Nuclear Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement).[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] This was in stark contrast to decades of U.S. policy, such as the stance taken by his predecessor, Bill Clinton, whose approach and response to India after the 1998 nuclear tests has been characterized as "sanctions and hectoring".[^cite_note-209]

Midway through Bush's second term, questions arose whether Bush was retreating from his freedom and democracy agenda, which was highlighted in policy changes toward some oil-rich former Soviet republics in central Asia.[^cite_note-210]

Bush signed the [Strategic Offensive Reductions Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Offensive_Reductions_Treaty) with Russia. He withdrew U.S. support for several international agreements, including, in 2002, the [Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Ballistic_Missile_Treaty) (ABM) with Russia.[^cite_note-211] This marked the first time in post-World War II history that the United States had withdrawn from a major international arms treaty.[^cite_note-acaabm-212] Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) stated that American withdrawal from the ABM Treaty was a mistake.[^cite_note-nyt-213]

Bush emphasized a careful approach to the conflict between Israel and the Palestinians; he denounced [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) leader [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) for his support of violence, but sponsored dialogues between Prime Minister [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) and Palestinian National Authority president [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md). Bush supported Sharon's unilateral disengagement plan, and lauded the democratic elections held in Palestine after Arafat's death.

Bush also expressed U.S. support for the defense of Taiwan following the stand-off in April 2001 with China over the [Hainan Island incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan_Island_incident), when an [EP-3E Aries II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EP-3E_Aries_II) surveillance aircraft collided with a [People's Liberation Army Air Force](381445.md) jet, leading to the detention of U.S. personnel. From 2003 to 2004, Bush authorized U.S. military intervention in Haiti and Liberia to protect U.S. interests. Bush condemned the [militia attacks in Darfur](5043637.md) and denounced the killings in Sudan as genocide.[^cite_note-214] Bush said an international peacekeeping presence was critical in Darfur, but he opposed referring the situation to the [International Criminal Court](14880.md).

On June 10, 2007, Bush met with [Albanian Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Prime_Minister) [Sali Berisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sali_Berisha) and became the first president to visit Albania.[^cite_note-215] He later voiced his support for the [independence of Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Kosovo).[^cite_note-216]

In early 2008, Bush vowed full support for admitting [Ukraine](31750.md) and [Georgia](48768.md) into [NATO](21133.md)[^cite_note-217] despite Russia's opposition to the further [enlargement of NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO).[^cite_note-218] During the [2008 Russo-Georgian diplomatic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russo-Georgian_diplomatic_crisis), Bush condemned Russia for recognizing the separatist government of [South Ossetia](318381.md).[^cite_note-219] When Russian troops invaded Georgia later that summer, Bush said: "Bullying and intimidation are not acceptable ways to conduct foreign policy in the 21st century."[^cite_note-220]

#### September 11, 2001, attacks

Main article: [September 11 attacks](5058690.md)

President Bush addressing the nation from the [White House](33057.md) at 8\:30 pm ET

The September 11 terrorist attacks were a major turning point in Bush's presidency. That evening, he addressed the nation from the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), promising a strong response to the attacks. He also emphasized the need for the nation to come together and comfort the families of the victims. Three days after the attacks, Bush visited [Ground Zero](67173.md) and met with then-New York City Mayor [Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani), firefighters, police officers, and volunteers. Bush addressed the gathering via a megaphone while standing on rubble: "I can hear you. The rest of the world hears you. And the people who knocked these buildings down will hear all of us soon."[^cite_note-221]

In a September 20 speech, Bush condemned [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) and his organization [al-Qaeda](1921.md), and issued an ultimatum to the Taliban regime in Afghanistan, where bin Laden was operating, to "hand over the terrorists, or ... share in their fate".[^cite_note-222] The Taliban's leader, [Mullah Omar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah_Omar), refused to hand over bin Laden.[^cite_note-Peter_Bergen-223]

The continued presence of U.S. troops in [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) after the 1991 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) was one of the stated motivations behind the September 11 attacks. In 2003, [the U.S. withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Southern_Watch) most of its troops from Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-224]

#### War on terror

Main article: [War on terror](13425800.md)

In Bush's September 20 speech, he declared that "our war on terror begins with Al Qaeda, but it does not end there."[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] In his January 29, [2002 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_State_of_the_Union_Address), he asserted that an "[axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)" consisting of [North Korea](21255.md), [Iran](14653.md), and [Ba'athist Iraq](26215470.md) was "arming to threaten the peace of the world" and "pose\[d] a grave and growing danger".[^cite_note-sotu2002-227] The Bush Administration asserted both a right and the intention to wage [preemptive war](384984.md), or [preventive war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_war).[^cite_note-228] This became the basis for the [Bush Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_Doctrine), which weakened the unprecedented levels of international and domestic support for the United States that had followed the September 11 attacks.[^cite_note-229]

Dissent and criticism of Bush's leadership in the war on terror increased as the war in Iraq continued.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] The Iraq war sparked many protests and riots in different parts of the world.[^cite_note-233] In 2006, a [National Intelligence Estimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Estimate) concluded that the Iraq War had become the "[cause célèbre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cause_c%C3%A9l%C3%A8bre) for [jihadists](16203.md)".[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

#### Afghanistan invasion

Main article: [War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](19666611.md)

On October 7, 2001, U.S. and British forces initiated bombing campaigns that led to the arrival of [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance) troops in [Kabul](16826.md) on November 13. The main goals of the war were to defeat the [Taliban](30635.md), drive [al-Qaeda](1921.md) out of Afghanistan, and capture key al-Qaeda leaders. In December 2001, the Pentagon reported that the Taliban had been defeated,[^cite_note-taliband-236] but cautioned that the war would go on to continue weakening Taliban and al-Qaeda leaders.[^cite_note-taliband-236] Later that month the UN had installed the [Afghan Transitional Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Transitional_Administration) chaired by [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai).[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238]

Efforts to kill or capture al-Qaeda leader Osama bin Laden failed as he escaped [a battle in December 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tora_Bora) in the mountainous region of [Tora Bora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tora_Bora), which the Bush Administration later acknowledged to have resulted from a failure to commit enough U.S. ground troops.[^cite_note-239] It was not until May 2011, two years after Bush left office, that [Bin Laden was killed by U.S. forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden) under the Obama administration.

Despite the initial success in driving the Taliban from power in Kabul, by early 2003 the Taliban was regrouping, amassing new funds and recruits.[^cite_note-240] The 2005 failure of [Operation Red Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Red_Wings) showed that the Taliban had returned.[^cite_note-241] In 2006, the [Taliban insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_insurgency) appeared larger, fiercer and better organized than expected, with large-scale allied offensives such as [Operation Mountain Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mountain_Thrust) attaining limited success.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] As a result, Bush deployed 3,500 additional troops to the country in March 2007.[^cite_note-245]

#### Iraq invasion

Main article: [Iraq War](5043324.md)



President Bush, with [Naval Flight Officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Flight_Officer) Lieutenant Ryan Philips, after landing on the USS *Abraham Lincoln* prior to his [Mission Accomplished speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Accomplished_speech), May 1, 2003

Beginning with his January 29, 2002 [State of the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_the_Union) address, Bush began publicly focusing attention on Iraq, which he labeled as part of an "[axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)" allied with terrorists and posing "a grave and growing danger" to U.S. interests through possession of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction).[^cite_note-sotu2002-227][^cite_note-246]

In the latter half of 2002, CIA [reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Estimate) contained assertions of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s intent of reconstituting nuclear weapons programs, not properly accounting for Iraqi [biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_warfare) and [chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_warfare), and that some Iraqi missiles had a range greater than allowed by the UN sanctions.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] Contentions that the Bush Administration manipulated or exaggerated the threat and evidence of Iraq's weapons of mass destruction capabilities would eventually become a major point of criticism for the president.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

In late 2002 and early 2003, Bush urged the United Nations to enforce Iraqi [disarmament](57979.md) mandates, precipitating a [diplomatic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_disarmament_crisis). In November 2002, [Hans Blix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Blix) and [Mohamed ElBaradei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_ElBaradei) led UN weapons inspectors in Iraq, but were advised by the U.S. to depart the country four days prior to the U.S. invasion, despite their requests for more time to complete their tasks.[^cite_note-251] The U.S. initially sought a [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) resolution authorizing the use of military force but dropped the bid for UN approval due to vigorous opposition from several countries.[^cite_note-252] The Bush administration's claim that the Iraq War was part of the war on terror had been [questioned and contested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_war_on_terror) by political analysts.[^cite_note-253]

More than 20 nations, most notably the United Kingdom, joined the United States in invading Iraq as part of the "[coalition of the willing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_of_the_willing)".[^cite_note-254] They launched the invasion on March 20, 2003. The Iraqi military was quickly defeated. The capital, [Baghdad](4492.md), fell on April 9, 2003. On May 1, Bush declared the end of major combat operations in Iraq. The initial success of U.S. operations increased his popularity, but the U.S. and allied forces faced a growing insurgency led by sectarian groups; Bush's "[Mission Accomplished](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Accomplished)" speech was later criticized as premature.[^cite_note-255] From 2004 until 2007, the situation in Iraq deteriorated further, with some observers arguing that there was a full-scale [civil war in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian_violence_in_Iraq_\(2006%E2%80%9307\)).[^cite_note-256] Bush's policies met with criticism, including demands domestically to set a timetable to withdraw troops from Iraq. The 2006 report of the bipartisan [Iraq Study Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Study_Group), led by [James Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baker), concluded that the situation in Iraq was "grave and deteriorating". While Bush admitted there were strategic mistakes made in regard to the stability of Iraq,[^cite_note-257] he maintained he would not change the overall Iraq strategy.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] According to [Iraq Body Count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Body_Count), some 251,000 Iraqis have been killed in the civil war following the U.S.-led invasion, including at least 163,841 civilians.[^cite_note-260]

In January 2005, elections recognized by the West as free and fair were held in Iraq for the first time in 50 years.[^cite_note-iraq_votes-261] This led to the election of [Jalal Talabani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_Talabani) as president and [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) as Prime Minister of Iraq. A referendum to approve a constitution in Iraq was held in October 2005, supported by most [Shiites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiite) and many [Kurds](17068.md).[^cite_note-262]

On January 10, 2007, Bush launched a [surge of 21,500 more troops for Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007), as well as a job program for Iraqis, more reconstruction proposals, and \$1.2 billion (equivalent to \$1.9 billion in 2025) for these programs.[^cite_note-263] On May 1, 2007, Bush used his second-ever veto to reject a bill setting a deadline for the withdrawal of U.S. troops,[^cite_note-264] saying the debate over the conflict was "understandable" but insisting that a continued U.S. presence there was crucial.[^cite_note-265]

In March 2008, Bush praised the Iraqi government's "bold decision" to launch the [Battle of Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Basra_\(2008\)) against the [Mahdi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_Army), calling it "a defining moment in the history of a free Iraq".[^cite_note-266] He said he would carefully weigh recommendations from his commanding General [David Petraeus](3223944.md) and Ambassador [Ryan Crocker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_Crocker) about how to proceed after the end of the military buildup in the summer of 2008. He also praised the Iraqis' legislative achievements, including a pension law, a revised de-Baathification law, a new budget, an amnesty law, and a provincial powers measure that, he said, set the stage for the [Iraqi elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Iraqi_governorate_elections).[^cite_note-267] By July 2008, American troop deaths had reached their lowest number since the war began,[^cite_note-cuts-nyt-268] and due to increased stability in Iraq, Bush withdrew additional American forces.[^cite_note-cuts-nyt-268] During Bush's last visit to Iraq in December 2008, Iraqi journalist [Muntadhar al-Zaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntadhar_al-Zaidi) [threw both of his shoes at him](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_shoeing_incident) during an official press conference with Iraqi Prime Minister [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md).[^cite_note-269] Al-Zaidi yelled that the shoes were a "farewell kiss" and "for the widows and orphans and all those killed in Iraq".[^cite_note-270]

In March 2010, the [Center for Public Integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Public_Integrity) released a report that Bush's administration had made more than 900 false pretenses in a two-year period about the alleged threat of Iraq against the United States, as his rationale to engage in war in Iraq.[^cite_note-271]

#### Surveillance

See also: [2010s global surveillance disclosures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_global_surveillance_disclosures)

Following the terrorist attacks of September 11, Bush issued an executive order that authorized the [President's Surveillance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Surveillance_Program).
