The new directive allowed the [National Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) to monitor communications between suspected terrorists outside the U.S. and parties within the U.S. without obtaining a warrant, which previously had been required by the [Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Intelligence_Surveillance_Act).[^cite_note-272] As of 2009, the other provisions of the program remained highly classified.[^cite_note-IG-273] Once the [Department of Justice](52563.md) [Office of Legal Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Legal_Counsel) questioned its original legal opinion that FISA did not apply in a time of war, the program was subsequently re-authorized by the President on the basis that the warrant requirements of FISA were implicitly superseded by the subsequent passage of the [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Terrorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Terrorists).[^cite_note-274] The program proved to be [controversial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_warrantless_surveillance_\(2001%E2%80%9307\)); critics of the administration and organizations such as the [American Bar Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Bar_Association) argued that it was illegal.[^cite_note-275] In August 2006, a U.S. district court judge ruled that the [NSA electronic surveillance program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_electronic_surveillance_program) was unconstitutional,[^cite_note-276] but on July 6, 2007, the ruling was [vacated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacated) by the [United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit](428328.md) on the grounds that the plaintiffs lacked [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_\(law\)).[^cite_note-277] On January 17, 2007, Attorney General [Alberto Gonzales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Gonzales) informed U.S. Senate leaders that the program would not be reauthorized by the President, but would be subjected to judicial oversight.[^cite_note-278] Later in 2007, the NSA launched a replacement for the program, referred to as [PRISM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRISM), which was subject to the oversight of the [United States Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Foreign_Intelligence_Surveillance_Court).[^cite_note-WaPo1-279] This program was not publicly revealed until reports by *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*[^cite_note-WaPo1-279] and *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*[^cite_note-Greenwald1-280] emerged in June 2013.[^cite_note-WaPo1-279]

#### Interrogation policies

See also: [U.S. Senate report on CIA torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Senate_report_on_CIA_torture) and [Torture Memos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_Memos)

Bush authorized the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) to use [waterboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterboarding) and several other "[enhanced interrogation techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_interrogation_techniques)" that several critics, including Barack Obama, would label as torture.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] Between 2002 and 2003, the CIA considered certain enhanced interrogation techniques, such as waterboarding, to be legal based on secret Justice Department legal opinions arguing that terror detainees were not protected by the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions)' ban on torture, which was described as "an unconstitutional infringement of the President's authority to conduct war".[^cite_note-cbs-waterboard-285][^cite_note-286] The CIA had exercised the technique on certain key terrorist suspects under authority given to it in the [Bybee Memo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bybee_Memo) from the Attorney General, though the memo was later withdrawn.[^cite_note-certain_olc-287] While not permitted by the [U.S. Army Field Manuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army_Field_Manuals) which assert "that harsh interrogation tactics elicit unreliable information",[^cite_note-cbs-waterboard-285] the Bush administration believed these enhanced interrogations "provided critical information" to preserve American lives.[^cite_note-288] Critics, such as former CIA officer [Bob Baer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Baer), have stated that information was suspect, "you can get anyone to confess to anything if the torture's bad enough."[^cite_note-289]

On October 17, 2006, Bush signed the [Military Commissions Act of 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Commissions_Act_of_2006) into law.[^cite_note-detainee-290] The new rule was enacted in the wake of the [Supreme Court's](31737.md) decision in *[Hamdan v. Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamdan_v._Rumsfeld)*, [548](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_cases,_volume_548) [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports) [557](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/548/557/) (2006),[^cite_note-291] which allowed the U.S. government to prosecute [unlawful enemy combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unlawful_enemy_combatants) by military commission rather than a standard trial. The law also denied the detainees access to *[habeas corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habeas_corpus)* and barred the torture of prisoners. The provision of the law allowed the president to determine what constitutes "torture".[^cite_note-detainee-290]

On March 8, 2008, Bush vetoed H.R. 2082,[^cite_note-292] a bill that would have expanded congressional oversight over the intelligence community and banned the use of waterboarding as well as other forms of interrogation not permitted under the [United States Army Field Manual on Human Intelligence Collector Operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FM_34-52_Intelligence_Interrogation), saying that "the bill Congress sent me would take away one of the most valuable tools in the War on Terror".[^cite_note-293] In April 2009, the ACLU sued and won release of the secret memos that had authorized the Bush administration's interrogation tactics.[^cite_note-Ass'tAtt'yGen'lBybeeMemo-294] One memo detailed specific interrogation tactics including a footnote that described waterboarding as torture and stated that the form of waterboarding used by the CIA was far more intense than authorized by the Justice Department.[^cite_note-295]

#### North Korea condemnation

Main article: [North Korea–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_relations)

Bush publicly condemned [Kim Jong-il](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Jong-il) of North Korea and identified North Korea as one of three states in an "[axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)". He said that "the United States of America will not permit the world's most dangerous regimes to threaten us with the world's most destructive weapons."[^cite_note-sotu2002-227] Within months, "both countries had walked away from their respective commitments under the U.S.–DPRK [Agreed Framework](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreed_Framework) of October 1994."[^cite_note-296] North Korea's October 9, 2006, [detonation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_North_Korean_nuclear_test) of a nuclear device further complicated Bush's foreign policy, which centered for both terms of his presidency on "\[preventing] the terrorists and regimes who seek chemical, biological, or nuclear weapons from threatening the United States and the world".[^cite_note-sotu2002-227] Bush condemned North Korea's position, reaffirmed his commitment to "a nuclear-free Korean Peninsula", and said that "transfer of nuclear weapons or material by North Korea to states or non-state entities would be considered a grave threat to the United States", for which North Korea would be held accountable.[^cite_note-White_House-2006-297] On May 7, 2007, North Korea agreed to shut down its nuclear reactors immediately pending the release of frozen funds held in a foreign bank account. This was a result of a series of three-way talks initiated by the United States and including China.[^cite_note-298] On September 2, 2007, North Korea agreed to disclose and dismantle all its nuclear programs by the end of 2007.[^cite_note-299] By May 2009, North Korea had restarted its nuclear program and threatened to attack South Korea.[^cite_note-300]

On June 22, 2010, Bush said: "While South Korea prospers, the people of North Korea have suffered profoundly," adding that communism had resulted in dire poverty, mass starvation, and brutal suppression. "In recent years," he went on to say, "the suffering has been compounded by the leader who wasted North Korea's precious few resources on personal luxuries and nuclear weapons programs."[^cite_note-301]

#### Syria sanctions

Bush expanded economic sanctions on Syria.[^cite_note-302] In 2003, Bush signed the [Syria Accountability Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Accountability_Act), which expanded sanctions on Syria. In early 2007, the [Treasury Department](53667.md), acting on a June 2005 [executive order](210541.md), froze American bank accounts of Syria's Higher Institute of Applied Science and Technology, Electronics Institute, and National Standards and Calibration Laboratory. Bush's order prohibits Americans from doing business with these institutions suspected of helping spread [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction)[^cite_note-303] and being supportive of terrorism.[^cite_note-304] Under separate executive orders signed by Bush in 2004 and later 2007, the Treasury Department froze the assets of two Lebanese and two Syrians, accusing them of activities to "undermine the legitimate political process in Lebanon" in November 2007. Those designated included: [Assaad Halim Hardan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assaad_Hardan), a member of Lebanon's parliament and former leader of the Syrian Socialist National Party; [Wi'am Wahhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiam_Wahhab), a former member of Lebanon's government (Minister of the Environment) under Prime Minister [Omar Karami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Karami) (2004–2005); [Hafiz Makhluf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_Makhlouf), a colonel and senior official in the [Syrian General Intelligence Directorate](19204147.md) and a cousin of Syrian president [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md); and [Muhammad Nasif Khayrbik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Nasif_Kheirbek), identified as a close adviser to Assad.[^cite_note-305]

#### AIDS Relief

In the [State of the Union address in January 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush outlined a five-year strategy for global emergency AIDS relief, the [President's Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Emergency_Plan_for_AIDS_Relief) (PEPFAR). Bush announced \$15 billion for this effort,[^cite_note-306] which directly supported life-saving antiretroviral treatment for more than 3.2 million men, women and children worldwide.[^cite_note-307] The U.S. government had spent some \$44 billion on the project since 2003 (a figure which includes \$7 billion contributed to the [Global Fund to Fight AIDS, Tuberculosis, and Malaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Global_Fund_to_Fight_AIDS,_Tuberculosis_and_Malaria), a multilateral organization),[^cite_note-308] which saved an estimated five million lives by 2013.[^cite_note-Foreign_Policy-309] *The New York Times* correspondent [Peter Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Baker_\(author\)) wrote in 2013 that "Bush did more to stop AIDS and more to help Africa than any president before or since."[^cite_note-Foreign_Policy-309] By 2023, PEPFAR was estimated to have saved over 25 million lives, alleviating the severity of the [HIV/AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_epidemic) especially in [Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md), and was called "George W. Bush's greatest accomplishment" by [Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\)).[^cite_note-PEPFAR-2023-310][^cite_note-Beauchamp-2023-311][^cite_note-Fauci-2018-312]

### Security incidents

Main article: [Security incidents involving George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_incidents_involving_George_W._Bush)

#### 2001 White House shooting

On February 7, 2001, while Bush was in the residence area of the White House, Robert W. Pickett, standing outside the perimeter fence, discharged a number of shots from a Taurus .38 Special revolver "in the general direction" of the White House. Pickett was shot in the knee by a [U.S. Secret Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Secret_Service) agent and arrested. Furthermore, he was initially charged with discharging a firearm during a crime, carrying a 10-year mandatory sentence, but following a plea agreement, Pickett instead entered a guilty plea to a firearms violation and an [Alford plea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alford_plea) to assaulting a federal officer. He was sentenced to three years at the [Federal Medical Center, Rochester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Medical_Center,_Rochester) followed by three years of probation.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]

#### 2005 Tbilisi grenade attack

On May 10, 2005, while President Bush was giving a speech in [Freedom Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Square,_Tbilisi), [Vladimir Arutyunian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Arutyunian), a native [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians) who was born to a family of ethnic [Armenians](387816.md), threw a live Soviet-made RGD-5 hand grenade toward the podium. It landed in the crowd about 61 feet (19 m) from the podium after hitting a girl, but it did not detonate because a red tartan handkerchief was wrapped tightly around it, preventing the safety lever from detaching.[^cite_note-FBIgrenade-317][^cite_note-318] Georgian president [Mikheil Saakashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Saakashvili) was seated nearby. After escaping that day, Arutyunian was arrested in July 2005. During his arrest, he killed an Interior Ministry agent. He was convicted in January 2006 and given a [life sentence](148681.md).[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-FBIgrenade-317]

#### 2008 Baghdad shoe-throwing incident

Main article: [George W. Bush shoe-throwing incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_shoe-throwing_incident)

On December 14, 2008, [Muntadhar al-Zaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntadhar_al-Zaidi), an Iraqi journalist, threw both of his shoes at Bush during a press conference in Baghdad. Bush was not injured, having ducked the pair of shoes.[^cite_note-Karadsheh-320] However, White House press secretary [Dana Perino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dana_Perino) received a bruise on her face after being hit by a microphone boom knocked over by security.[^cite_note-321] Al-Zaidi received a three-year prison sentence which was reduced to one year. On September 15, 2009, he was released early for good behavior.[^cite_note-Karadsheh-320]

### Judicial appointments

#### Supreme Court

Main article: [George W. Bush Supreme Court candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Supreme_Court_candidates)

On July 19, 2005, following the retirement of [Associate Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) [Sandra Day O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_Day_O'Connor) on July 1, Bush nominated federal appellate judge [John Roberts](1928850.md) as her replacement; however, following the death of Chief Justice [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist) on September 3, the still-pending nomination was withdrawn on September 5, with Bush instead nominating Roberts to be the next [Chief Justice of the United States](31739.md). He was confirmed by the Senate on September 29, 2005.[^cite_note-roll_call_roberts-322]

On October 3, 2005, Bush nominated [White House Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Counsel) [Harriet Miers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Miers) to succeed O'Connor; however, Miers withdrew her nomination on October 27 after encountering significant opposition from both parties, who found her to be ill-prepared and uninformed on the law.[^cite_note-greenburg-102]<sup>: 278 </sup> Finally, on October 31, Bush nominated federal appellate judge [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito), who was confirmed by the Senate to replace O'Connor on January 31, 2006.[^cite_note-323]

#### Other courts

Main article: [List of federal judges appointed by George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_George_W._Bush)

In addition to his two Supreme Court appointments, Bush appointed 62 judges to the [United States courts of appeals](70228.md) and 261 judges to the [United States district courts](70235.md).

## Post-presidency (2009–present)

Main article: [Post-presidency of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-presidency_of_George_W._Bush)

### Residence



Former president George W. Bush and his wife being escorted to a waiting helicopter by President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) and First Lady [Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama) on January 20, 2009

After the [inauguration of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Barack_Obama), Bush and his family flew from [Andrews Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrews_Air_Force_Base) to a homecoming celebration in [Midland, Texas](151222.md), and then returned to their ranch in [Crawford, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crawford,_Texas).[^cite_note-leaving-324] They bought a home in the [Preston Hollow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston_Hollow) neighborhood of [Dallas](53838.md) where they live.[^cite_note-325]

Bush made regular appearances at various events throughout the Dallas–Fort Worth area, including the opening coin toss at the [Dallas Cowboys](8121.md)' first game in the new [Cowboys Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboys_Stadium) in [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas)[^cite_note-326] and an April 2009 [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) game, where he thanked the people of Dallas for helping him settle in, which was met with a standing ovation.[^cite_note-327] He also attended every home playoff game during the [Rangers' 2010 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Texas_Rangers_season) and, accompanied by his father, threw out the ceremonial first pitch at the [Rangers Ballpark in Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangers_Ballpark_in_Arlington) for Game 4 of the [2010 World Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_World_Series) on October 31.[^cite_note-328] He also threw the first pitch in Game 1 of the [2023 World Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_World_Series).[^cite_note-329]

On August 6, 2013, Bush was successfully treated for a [coronary artery blockage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronary_artery_blockage) with a [stent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronary_stent). The blockage had been found during an [annual medical examination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_medical_examination).[^cite_note-330]

In reaction to the [2016 shooting of Dallas police officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_shooting_of_Dallas_police_officers), Bush said: "Laura and I are heartbroken by the heinous acts of violence in our city last night. Murdering the innocent is always evil, never more so than when the lives taken belong to those who protect our families and communities."[^cite_note-331]

### Publications and appearances

Since leaving office, Bush has kept a relatively low profile.[^cite_note-332] Bush has spoken in favor of increased global participation of women in politics and societal matters in foreign countries.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

In March 2009, he delivered his first post-presidency speech in [Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary), Alberta,[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] appeared via video on *[The Colbert Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Colbert_Report)* during which he praised U.S. troops for earning a "special place in American history",[^cite_note-337] and attended the funeral of Senator [Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy).[^cite_note-pols-338] Bush made his debut as a motivational speaker on October 26 at the "Get Motivated" seminar in Dallas.[^cite_note-339] In the aftermath of the [Fort Hood shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Fort_Hood_shooting) on November 5, 2009, the Bushes paid an undisclosed visit to the survivors and the victims' families the day following the shooting, having contacted the base commander requesting that the visit be private and not involve press coverage.[^cite_note-340]



Bush eulogizing his father at the National Cathedral on December 5, 2018

Bush released his memoirs, *[Decision Points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decision_Points)*, on November 9, 2010.[^cite_note-341] During a pre-release appearance promoting the book, Bush said he considered his biggest accomplishment to be keeping "the country safe amid a real danger", and his greatest failure to be his inability to secure the passage of [Social Security reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_reform).[^cite_note-342] He also made news defending his administration's enhanced interrogation techniques, specifically the [waterboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterboarding) of [Khalid Sheikh Mohammed](190884.md), saying, "I'd do it again to save lives."[^cite_note-343]

In 2012, he wrote the foreword to *[The 4% Solution: Unleashing the Economic Growth America Needs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_4%25_Solution:_Unleashing_the_Economic_Growth_America_Needs)*, an economics book published by the [George W. Bush Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Presidential_Center).[^cite_note-lubbockfourpercent-344][^cite_note-soniasmithfourpercent-345] He also presented the book at the [Parkland Memorial Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parkland_Memorial_Hospital) in Dallas, Texas.[^cite_note-booktvfourpercent-346] Bush did not physically appear in that year's [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Republican_National_Convention) (where [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney) obtained the party's nomination for president), instead appearing in a videotape, in which he –alongside his father and immediate family– explained his reasons for supporting Romney.[^cite_note-347]

Bush appeared on [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC)'s *[The Tonight Show with Jay Leno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tonight_Show_with_Jay_Leno)* on November 19, 2013, along with his wife Laura. When asked by Leno why he does not comment publicly about the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration), Bush said: "I don't think it's good for the country to have a former president criticize his successor."[^cite_note-348] Despite this statement, Bush vocally disagreed with Obama's withdrawal of U.S. troops from Iraq in 2011, calling it a "strategic blunder".[^cite_note-349] In December, Bush traveled with President Obama to the memorial service of South African president and civil rights leader [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela).[^cite_note-350] There, they joined former presidents Bill Clinton and Jimmy Carter.[^cite_note-351]

Alongside the [2014 United States–Africa Leaders Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Africa_Leaders_Summit_2014), Bush, [Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama), the State Department, and the [George W. Bush Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Institute) hosted a daylong forum on education and health with the spouses of the African leaders attending the summit. Bush urged African leaders to avoid discriminatory laws that make the treatment of [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) more difficult.[^cite_note-352] On November 2, Bush spoke at an event to 200 business and civic leaders at the George W. Bush Presidential Library and Museum to raise awareness of the upcoming [Museum of the Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Bible) in Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-bushmuseumofthebible-353][^cite_note-354] On November 11, Bush published a biography of [his father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) titled *[41: A Portrait of My Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41:_A_Portrait_of_My_Father)*.[^cite_note-355]

In an interview published by *[Israel Hayom](12524641.md)* magazine on June 12, 2015, Bush said that "boots on the ground" would be needed to defeat the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) (ISIS). He added that people had said during his presidency that he should withdraw American troops from Iraq, but he chose the opposite, sending 30,000 more troops to defeat [Al Qaeda in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Qaeda_in_Iraq), and that they indeed were defeated. Bush was also asked about Iran but declined to answer, stating that any answer he gives would be interpreted as undermining Obama.[^cite_note-IsraelHayom-356]

During the early stages of the [2016 Republican presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), Bush spoke and campaigned for his brother [Jeb Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeb_Bush) at a South Carolina rally.[^cite_note-357] However, the party's nomination eventually went to [Donald Trump](4848272.md), whom Bush [refused to endorse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Republicans_who_opposed_the_Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign).[^cite_note-358] Furthermore, he did not attend [the party's convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_National_Convention).[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] On the eve of Trump's nomination, it was reported that Bush had privately expressed concern about the current direction of the Republican Party, telling a group of his former aides and advisors that "I'm worried that I will be the last Republican president."[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] According to a spokesperson for the Bush family, he did not vote for either Trump or [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) in the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), instead choosing to leave his presidential ballot blank.[^cite_note-363]

After the 2016 elections, Bush, his father, and his brother Jeb called Trump on the phone to congratulate him on his victory.[^cite_note-364] On January 20, 2017, Bush and his wife attended [Trump's first inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump). Images of Bush struggling to put on a rain [poncho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poncho) during the ceremony became an [internet meme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_meme).[^cite_note-365] While leaving the event, Bush allegedly described the ceremony, and Trump's inaugural address in particular, as "some weird shit".[^cite_note-366]

In February 2017, Bush released a book of his own portraits of veterans called *[Portraits of Courage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portraits_of_Courage)*.[^cite_note-nbcpresidentwbush-367] In August, following the white nationalist [Unite the Right rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Right_rally), Bush and his father released a joint statement condemning the violence and ideologies present there.[^cite_note-368] Subsequently, Bush gave a speech in New York where he noted of the current political climate, "Bigotry seems emboldened. Our politics seems more vulnerable to conspiracy theories and outright fabrication." He continued, "Bigotry in any form is blasphemy against the American creed and it means the very identity of our nation depends on the passing of civic ideals to the next generation", while urging citizens to oppose threats to American democracy and be positive role models for young people.[^cite_note-Trumprebuke-369] The speech was widely interpreted as a denouncement of Donald Trump and [his ideologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Donald_Trump), despite Bush not mentioning Trump by name.[^cite_note-Trumprebuke-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]

On September 1, 2018, Bush and his wife attended the funeral of [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) at the [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral) in Washington, D.C. where Bush delivered remarks at the service.[^cite_note-373] On November 30, his father [died at his home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_George_H._W._Bush). Shortly before his death, Bush was able to talk with his father on the phone; his father responded with what would be his last words, "I love you too".[^cite_note-374] Bush attended his father's funeral on December 5, delivering a eulogy.[^cite_note-375] In May 2019, on the tenth anniversary of former South Korean president [Roh Moo-hyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roh_Moo-hyun)'s death, Bush visited South Korea to pay his respects to Roh, delivering a short eulogy.[^cite_note-Maresca-2019-376]

On June 1, 2020, Bush released a statement addressing the [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) and the subsequent nationwide [reaction and protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests).[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] In the statement, Bush wrote that he and former first lady [Laura Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Bush) "are anguished by the brutal suffocation of George Floyd and disturbed by the injustice and fear that suffocate our country".[^cite_note-379] He also elaborated on the racial injustices perpetrated by the police, saying that "it is time for America to examine our tragic failures" and adding "Many doubt the justice of our country, and with good reason. Black people see the repeated violation of their rights without an urgent and adequate response from American institutions".[^cite_note-380] On July 30, Bush and his wife, along with former presidents Bill Clinton and Barack Obama, attended and spoke at the funeral for civil rights leader and congressman [John Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis) at Ebenezer Baptist Church in Atlanta.[^cite_note-381]

Bush did not give any endorsements during the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md),[^cite_note-382] but held a virtual fundraiser for U.S. senators [Susan Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Collins_\(politician\)) (R-ME), [Cory Gardner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cory_Gardner) (R-CO), [Martha McSally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martha_McSally) (R-AZ), and [Thom Tillis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thom_Tillis) (R-NC). All four were [up for reelection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Senate_elections) and were struggling in the polls.[^cite_note-383] He also did not attend the [2020 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_National_Convention) where President Trump was re-nominated.[^cite_note-384] In April 2021, Bush told *[People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_\(magazine\))* magazine that he did not vote for either Trump or [Joe Biden](145422.md) in the general election. Instead, he wrote in [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice), who served as his national security advisor from 2001 to 2005 and as his secretary of state from 2005 to 2009.[^cite_note-385] When the election was called for Biden, Bush congratulated him and his running mate [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris). He also congratulated Trump and his supporters "on a hard-fought campaign". Bush's outreach to Biden was notable since Republican candidate Donald Trump had not yet conceded. Bush then issued a statement saying that while Trump was within his rights to call for recounts, he believed the election was "fundamentally fair" and that "its outcome is clear", and said he would offer Biden "my prayers for his success, and my pledge to help in any way I can", as he had for Trump and Obama.[^cite_note-Bradner-386][^cite_note-Baker2020-387][^cite_note-Subramanian-388]



George W. Bush and Laura at the inauguration of [Joe Biden](145422.md) on January 20, 2021

On January 6, 2021, following the [U.S. Capitol attack](66307490.md), Bush denounced the violence and attack alongside the three other living former presidents, Obama, Clinton, and Carter,[^cite_note-389] releasing a statement saying that "this is how election results are disputed in a [banana republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana_republic), not our [democratic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_republic)",[^cite_note-390] and that "it is a sickening and heartbreaking sight".[^cite_note-391] He also echoed president-elect Biden's message saying that what occurred at the Capitol was an "insurrection".[^cite_note-392] On January 20, Bush and his wife attended [Biden's inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden).[^cite_note-393]

Bush opposed President Biden's [withdrawal of American troops from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Afghanistan_\(2020%E2%80%932021\)), saying that the withdrawal made him "concerned" and that it had the potential to "create a vacuum, and into that vacuum is likely to come people who treat women as second-class citizens".[^cite_note-394] During an interview with [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle) on July 14, 2021, Bush reaffirmed his opposition to the troop withdrawal, calling the plan "a mistake".[^cite_note-395]

On September 11, 2021, the 20th anniversary of the September 11 attacks, Bush gave a speech at the [Flight 93 National Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_93_National_Memorial), praising the heroism of the people on [Flight 93](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_93) and the spirit of America. He also said that he "saw millions of people instinctively grab for a neighbor's hand and rally to the cause of one another. That is the America I know."[^cite_note-396]

Bush condemned [the assassination attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Donald_Trump_in_Pennsylvania) on then-former president Trump, who was the presumptive Republican nominee, on July 13, 2024, calling it "cowardly" and applauding the Secret Service's response.[^cite_note-397] However, Bush did not participate in that year's [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_National_Convention), which took place two days after the attempt, and where Trump was nominated for a third time.[^cite_note-398] He also chose not to endorse any candidate in the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-399] After the election was called for Trump, Bush offered his congratulations to him and his running mate [JD Vance](53396477.md). He stated that the large turnout for the election was a "sign of the health of our republic and the strength of our democratic institutions". He also congratulated Biden and Harris on their years of public office.[^cite_note-400] On January 20, 2025, Bush and his wife attended [Trump's second inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-401] On September 10, 2025, Bush released a statement condemning the [assassination of Charlie Kirk](81040139.md). He wrote "Today, a young man was murdered in cold blood while expressing his political views. It happened on a college campus, where the open exchange of opposing ideas should be sacrosanct." He also elaborated on [political violence in the United States](73757192.md): "Violence and vitriol must be purged from the public square. Members of other political parties are not our enemies; they are our fellow citizens. May God bless [Charlie Kirk](53245776.md) and his family, and may God guide America toward civility."[^cite_note-402]



Bush attending Cheney's funeral on November 20, 2025

After [Peyton Manning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peyton_Manning) tried to get Bush to appear on *[Monday Night Football with Peyton and Eli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monday_Night_Football_with_Peyton_and_Eli)* for five years, Bush relented. On the show on November 17, 2025, Bush said that he grew up a [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros) and a [Houston Oilers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Oilers) fan watching [Earl Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Campbell) and [Dan Pastorini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Pastorini) (he called Pastorini, Dante Pastorini) play for the Oilers. Bush also mentioned [Billy "White Shoes" Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_%22White_Shoes%22_Johnson). Bush said that he and his group bought the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) and he moved to Dallas the same year that [Jerry Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Jones) bought the [Dallas Cowboys](8121.md). At that time, Bush got to know Jones well and switched his allegiance to the Cowboys. Bush mentioned that his father liked the [Houston Texans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Texans) and the Astros, calling him a loyal Houston guy. He said [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) was a friend of the Texans' owner. When Peyton Manning asked George W. Bush if he thought that he would have a job in public service, Bush answered no, adding that he would have behaved a lot better in college if he had known. "I admired my dad a lot... he never said, this is what you have to do with your life, he just said, here's this kind of way I'd like you to live, honesty and decency and compassion, but you be what you want to be... it took me a while to figure out what I wanted to be, by the way."

Bush talked about growing up in [Midland, Texas](151222.md). He said, "You either pick art or football. I chose football." He has run [marathons](49132.md). [Willie Mays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willie_Mays) has been a hero of his.[^cite_note-403] On November 20, 2025, Bush and his wife attended the funeral of his former vice president Cheney at the [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral) in Washington, D.C., where Bush spoke.[^cite_note-404] Bush and his wife attended the dedication ceremony of the [Barack Obama Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_Presidential_Center) on June 18, 2026.[^cite_note-405]

### Collaborations

President Obama with former presidents [Clinton](3356.md) and Bush present the [Clinton Bush Haiti Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Bush_Haiti_Fund) after the 2010 earthquake.

In January 2010, at President Obama's request, Bush and Bill Clinton established the [Clinton Bush Haiti Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Bush_Haiti_Fund) to raise contributions for relief and recovery efforts after the [2010 Haiti earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake) earlier that month.[^cite_note-406] On May 2, 2011, President Obama called Bush, who was at a restaurant with his wife, to inform him that [Osama bin Laden had been killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Osama_bin_Laden).[^cite_note-407] The Bushes joined the Obamas in New York City to mark the tenth anniversary of the September 11, 2001, terrorist attacks. At the Ground Zero memorial, Bush read [a letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bixby_letter) that President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) wrote to a widow who had lost five sons during the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-408]

On September 7, 2017, Bush partnered with former presidents [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), Bill Clinton, and Barack Obama to work with [One America Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_America_Appeal) to help the victims of [Hurricane Harvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Harvey) and [Hurricane Irma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Irma) in the [Gulf Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coast) and [Texas](29810.md) communities.[^cite_note-409] Over the years, President Bush has had a good-natured friendship with Michelle Obama. "President Bush and I, we are forever seatmates because of protocol, and that's how we sit at all the official functions," Obama told the *[Today Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Today_\(American_TV_program\))*. "He's my partner in crime at every major thing where all the 'formers' gather. So we're together all the time."[^cite_note-410] Bush famously passed mints to Obama during the McCain funeral in September 2018 and gave them to her again during the [funeral of his father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_George_H._W._Bush) in December 2018.[^cite_note-411]

### Art

After serving as president, Bush began painting as a [hobby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hobby) after reading [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill)'s essay "Painting as a Pastime". Subjects have included people, dogs, and [still life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Still_life).[^cite_note-412] He has also painted self-portraits and portraits of world leaders, including [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair).[^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415] In February 2017, Bush released a book of portraits of veterans, *[Portraits of Courage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portraits_of_Courage)*.[^cite_note-nbcpresidentwbush-367] The net proceeds from his book are donated to the [George W. Bush Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Presidential_Center). In May 2019, on the tenth anniversary of former South Korean president [Roh Moo-hyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roh_Moo-hyun)'s death, George Bush drew a portrait of Roh to give to his family.[^cite_note-Maresca-2019-376]

## Cultural and political image

Main article: [Public image of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_George_W._Bush)

See also: [Efforts to impeach George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_impeach_George_W._Bush)

### Image

Bush's upbringing in [West Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Texas), his accent, his [vacations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Presidential_vacations) to his Texas ranch, and his penchant for country metaphors contribute to his folksy, American cowboy image.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-BBCcowboy-417] "I think people look at him and think [John Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wayne)", said [Piers Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piers_Morgan), editor of the British *[Daily Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Mirror)*.[^cite_note-418]

Bush has been [parodied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parodied) by the media,[^cite_note-419] comedians, and other politicians.[^cite_note-420] Detractors tended to cite linguistic errors made by Bush during his public speeches, which are colloquially referred to as [Bushisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushism).[^cite_note-421]

In contrast to his father, who was perceived as having trouble with an overarching unifying theme, Bush embraced larger visions and was seen as a man of larger ideas who took significant risks.[^cite_note-422]

[Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) wrote in 2010 that the caricature of Bush as being dumb is "ludicrous" and that Bush is "very smart".[^cite_note-423] In an interview with *[Playboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playboy)*, *The New York Times* columnist [David Brooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brooks_\(journalist\)) said Bush "was 60 IQ points smarter in private than he was in public. He doesn't want anybody to think he's smarter than they are, so he puts on a Texas act."[^cite_note-rogers2012-424]

### Job approval



[Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll)/*[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)* Bush public opinion polling from February 2001 to January 2009:

&#x20; Approve

&#x20; Disapprove

&#x20; Unsure

Bush began his presidency with [approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_approval_rating) near 60 percent.[^cite_note-425] After the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), Bush gained an approval rating of 90 percent,[^cite_note-426] maintaining 80–90 percent approval for four months after the attacks. It remained over 50 percent during most of his first term[^cite_note-427] and then fell to as low as 19 percent in his second term.[^cite_note-jobapp19-428]

In 2000 and again in 2004, *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* magazine named George W. Bush as its [Person of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Person_of_the_Year), a title awarded to someone who the editors believe "has done the most to influence the events of the year".[^cite_note-429] In May 2004, [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) reported that 89 percent of the Republican electorate approved of Bush.[^cite_note-430] However, the support waned due mostly to a minority of Republicans' frustration with him on issues of spending, illegal immigration, and Middle Eastern affairs.[^cite_note-431]

Within the United States armed forces, according to an unscientific survey, the president was strongly supported in the 2004 presidential elections.[^cite_note-military_support-432] While 73 percent of military personnel said they would vote for Bush, 18 percent preferred his Democratic rival, [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry).[^cite_note-military_support-432] According to [Peter Feaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Feaver), a [Duke University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_University) political scientist who has studied the political leanings of the U.S. military, members of the armed services supported Bush because they found him more likely than Kerry to complete the War in Iraq.[^cite_note-military_support-432]

Bush's approval rating surged to 74 percent at the beginning of the [Iraq War](5043324.md), up 19 points from his pre-war rating of 55 percent.[^cite_note-433] Bush's approval rating went below the 50 percent mark in [AP](18935732.md)-[Ipsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos) polling in December 2004.[^cite_note-Taipei_Times-434] Thereafter, his approval ratings and approval of his handling of domestic and foreign policy issues steadily dropped. After his re-election in 2004, Bush received increasingly heated criticism from across the political spectrum[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436][^cite_note-Baker-437] for his handling of the Iraq War, his [response to Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_effects_of_Hurricane_Katrina),[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439][^cite_note-PBS-2006-440] and to the [Abu Ghraib prisoner abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_prisoner_abuse), [NSA warrantless surveillance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_warrantless_surveillance), the [Plame affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plame_affair), and [Guantanamo Bay detention camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp) controversies.[^cite_note-Unchecked_and_Unbalanced-441]

Amid this criticism, the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) regained control of Congress in the [2006 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_midterm_elections). Polls conducted in 2006 showed an average approval rating of 37 percent for Bush,[^cite_note-PollingReport-442] the lowest for any second-term president at that point in his term since [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman) in March 1951 (when Truman's approval rating was 28 percent),[^cite_note-Taipei_Times-434][^cite_note-443] which contributed to what Bush called the "thumping" of the Republican Party in the 2006 elections.[^cite_note-444] Throughout most of 2007, Bush's approval rating hovered in the mid-thirties;[^cite_note-445] the average for his entire second term was 37 percent, according to Gallup.[^cite_note-446]



Protest against the [Iraq War](5043324.md) in New London, Connecticut on May 23, 2007

By the beginning of 2008, his final year in office, Bush's approval rating had dropped to a low of just 19 percent, largely from the loss of support among Republicans.[^cite_note-jobapp19-428] Commenting on his low poll numbers and accusations of being "the worst president",[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448] Bush would say, "I make decisions on what I think is right for the United States based upon principles. I frankly don't give a damn about the polls."[^cite_note-Fox_News-449]

There were [calls for Bush's impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_impeach_George_W._Bush), though most polls showed a plurality of Americans would not support such an action.[^cite_note-450] The arguments offered for impeachment usually centered on the NSA warrantless surveillance controversy,[^cite_note-451] the Bush administration's justification for the war in Iraq, and alleged violations of the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions).[^cite_note-452] Representative [Dennis Kucinich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Kucinich) (D-[OH](22199.md)), who had run against Bush during the 2004 presidential campaign, introduced 35 articles of impeachment on the floor of [the House of Representatives](19468510.md) against Bush on June 9, 2008, but [Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) (D-[CA](5407.md)) declared that impeachment was "off the table".[^cite_note-453]

In April 2008, Bush's disapproval ratings reached the highest ever recorded for any president in the 70-year history of the [Gallup poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll), with 69 percent of those polled disapproving of the job Bush was doing as president and 28 percent approving – although the majority (66 percent) of Republicans still approved of his job performance.[^cite_note-USAToday_April_21,_2008-bushrating-454]

In polls conducted in the fall, just before the 2008 election, his approval ratings remained at record lows of 19 to 20 percent,[^cite_note-CBSNYTfinalpolls-455][^cite_note-ARGbushpolls-456] while his disapproval ratings ranged from 67 percent to as high as 75 percent.[^cite_note-ARGbushpolls-456][^cite_note-PollingReport-442] In polling conducted January 9–11, 2009, his final job approval rating by Gallup was 34 percent, which placed him on par with [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) and [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman), the other presidents whose final Gallup ratings measured in the low 30s ([Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s final Gallup approval rating was even lower, at 24 percent).[^cite_note-saadgall-457] According to a [CBS News](359551.md)/*New York Times* poll conducted January 11–15, 2009, Bush's final approval rating in office was 22 percent, the lowest in American history.[^cite_note-CBSNYTfinalpolls-455]

### Foreign perceptions



Countries with a [U.S. military presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_military_deployments) in 2007

Bush was criticized internationally and targeted by the global [anti-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-war) and [anti-globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-globalization) movements for his administration's foreign policy.[^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459] Views of him within the international community – even in France, a close ally of the United States – were more negative than those of most previous American presidents.[^cite_note-460]

Bush was described as having especially close personal relationships with Tony Blair of the United Kingdom and [Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fox) of Mexico, although formal relations were sometimes strained.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] Other leaders, such as [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai) of Afghanistan,[^cite_note-464] [Yoweri Museveni](293409.md) of Uganda,[^cite_note-465] [José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero](19614411.md) of Spain,[^cite_note-466] and [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) of Venezuela,[^cite_note-467] openly criticized the president. Later in Bush's presidency, tensions arose between him and [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), which led to a cooling of their relationship.[^cite_note-LeeryofPutin-468]

In 2006, most respondents in 18 of 21 countries surveyed around the world were found to hold an unfavorable opinion of Bush. Respondents indicated that they judged his administration as negative for world security.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470] In 2007, the [Pew Global Attitudes Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Global_Attitudes_Project) reported that during the Bush presidency, attitudes towards the United States, and towards Americans, became less favorable around the world.[^cite_note-471] The [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center)'s 2007 Global Attitudes poll found that in only nine countries of 47 did most respondents express "a lot of confidence" or "some confidence" in Bush: Ethiopia, Ghana, India, Israel, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Mali, Nigeria, and Uganda.[^cite_note-pew_research-472] A March 2007 survey of public opinion in six Arab nations conducted by Zogby International and the [University of Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Maryland) found that Bush was the most disliked world leader.[^cite_note-473]

During a June 2007 visit to the predominantly Muslim[^cite_note-474] [Albania](738.md), Bush was greeted enthusiastically. Albania has a population of 2.8 million,[^cite_note-475] has troops in both Iraq and Afghanistan, and the country's government is highly supportive of American foreign policy.[^cite_note-albania-476] A huge image of the President was hung in the middle of the capital city of [Tirana](31075.md) flanked by Albanian and American flags while a local street was named after him.[^cite_note-477][^cite_note-Albanian_Street_Named_After_George_W-2007-478] A shirt-sleeved statue of Bush was unveiled in [Fushë-Krujë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fush%C3%AB-Kruj%C3%AB), a few kilometers northwest of Tirana.[^cite_note-479] The Bush administration's support for the unilateral [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Kosovo_declaration_of_independence) of Albanian-majority [Kosovo](17391.md), while endearing him to the Albanians, troubled U.S. relations with Serbia, leading to the February 2008 torching of the U.S. embassy in [Belgrade](55904.md).[^cite_note-480]

## Legacy

See also: [Bush Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_Doctrine) and [Fictionalized portrayals of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fictionalized_portrayals_of_George_W._Bush)



[George W. Bush Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Presidential_Center), on the campus of [Southern Methodist University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Methodist_University)

Bush's legacy continues to develop, as time allows the development of a more nuanced historical perspective. Supporters credit his counterterrorism policies with preventing another major terrorist attack in the U.S. after September 11 and praise policies such as the [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) prescription drug benefit and the AIDS relief program known as [PEPFAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PEPFAR). Critics point to his handling of the [Iraq War](5043324.md), specifically the failure to find [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) after claiming they were in Iraq, as well as Bush's handling of [tax policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_States), [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina), [climate change](5042951.md) and the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), as proof he was unfit to be president.[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] [Ben Ferencz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Ferencz), chief prosecutor for the US Army at the [Nuremberg Trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Trials), stated that Bush likely committed [war crimes](48626.md) in relation to the Iraq War.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485][^cite_note-486]

Several historians and commentators hold that Bush was one of the most consequential presidents in American history. Julian Zelizer described Bush's presidency as a "transformative" one, and said that "some people hate him, some people love him, but I do think he'll have a much more substantive perception as time goes on".[^cite_note-487] Bryon Williams of *[The Huffington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Huffington_Post)* referred to Bush as "the most noteworthy president since [FDR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FDR)" and said the [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act) "increased authority of the executive branch at the expense of judicial opinions about when searches and seizures are reasonable" as evidence.[^cite_note-488] Bush's administration presided over the largest tax cuts since the [presidency of Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan),[^cite_note-489] and his homeland security reforms proved to be the most significant expansion of the federal government since the [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society).[^cite_note-490]

Some scholars have situated Bush's presidency as the extension of the [Reagan era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_era) into the 21st century[^cite_note-491] and the consolidation of American [neoconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoconservatism). Likewise, scholars have argued that his decisions not only reflected neoconservative thinking but institutionalized it, transforming those ideas into official strategic doctrine and giving neoconservatism unprecedented influence by turning ideas which had previously circulated mainly in think tanks and intellectual circles into official state policy.[^cite_note-492]

Bush has been [widely portrayed in film and television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fictionalized_portrayals_of_George_W._Bush), during and after his presidency.[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495] He has had [various nicknames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nicknames_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#George_W._Bush) including "Dubya",[^cite_note-496] "GWB"[^cite_note-497] and "Shrub".[^cite_note-498]

### Reception

The Bush presidency has been ranked as below-average in [surveys of presidential scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States) published in the late 2000s and 2010s.[^cite_note-US_News-499][^cite_note-Austin-500][^cite_note-Mann-501]

A 2010 [Siena Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siena_Research_Institute) survey of the opinions of historians, political scientists, and presidential scholars ranked him 39th out of 43 presidents. The survey respondents gave Bush low ratings on his handling of the economy, communication, ability to compromise, foreign policy, and intelligence.[^cite_note-Siena_2010-502] Bush said in 2013, "Ultimately history will judge the decisions I made, and I won't be around because it will take time for the objective historians to show up. So I am pretty comfortable with it. I did what I did."[^cite_note-503] C-SPAN's 2021 survey of historians ranked Bush as the 29th-best president; Bush had initially been ranked 36th in 2009.[^cite_note-CSPAN_Survey-504]

Among the public, his reputation has improved in the U.S. since his presidency ended in 2009. In 2012, Gallup reported that "Americans still rate George W. Bush among the worst presidents, though their views have become more positive in the three years since he left office."[^cite_note-gallup0212-505] A poll conducted in 2013 marked the first time recorded by Gallup that his ratings were more positive than negative, with 49 percent viewing him favorably compared to 46 percent unfavorably.[^cite_note-506] Other pollsters have noted similar trends of slight improvement  in Bush's personal favorability since the end of his presidency.[^cite_note-pollingreport.com-507] In April 2013, Bush's approval rating stood at 47 percent approval and 50 percent disapproval in a poll jointly conducted for *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* and [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company), his highest approval rating since December 2005.[^cite_note-Mali-2013-508] Bush had achieved notable gains among seniors, non-college whites, and moderate and conservative Democrats since leaving office, although majorities disapproved of his handling of the economy (53 percent) and the Iraq War (57 percent).[^cite_note-George_W\._Bush's_approval_rating_just_hit_a_7-year_high._Here's_how\.-509] His 47 percent approval rating was equal to President Obama's in the same polling period.[^cite_note-Mali-2013-508] A [CNN](62028.md) poll conducted that same month found that 55 percent of Americans said Bush's presidency had been a failure, with opinions divided along party lines, and 43 percent of independents calling it a success.[^cite_note-510] Bush's public image saw greater improvement in 2017, with a [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) survey showing a 51 percent of favorability among Democrats.[^cite_note-511] A 2018 CNN poll subsequently found that 61 percent of respondents held a favorable view of Bush, an increase of nine points from 2015.[^cite_note-512] The improvement has been interpreted as Democrats viewing him more favorably in response to [Donald Trump's first presidency](52231341.md),[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] an assessment that has also been expressed by Bush himself.[^cite_note-516]

### Honors and awards

See also: [List of honors and awards received by George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_honors_and_awards_received_by_George_W._Bush)

A street in [Tirana, Albania](31075.md), located directly outside the [Albanian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Parliament) and formerly known as *Rruga Punëtorët e Rilindjes*, was renamed after Bush a few days before he made the first-ever visit by an American president to Albania in June 2007.[^cite_note-Albanian_Street_Named_After_George_W-2007-478] In 2012, Estonian president [Toomas Hendrik Ilves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toomas_Hendrik_Ilves) awarded Bush the [Order of the Cross of Terra Mariana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Cross_of_Terra_Mariana) for his work in expanding NATO.[^cite_note-517] Two elementary schools are named after him: one in [Stockton Unified School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockton_Unified_School_District) in [Stockton, California](99302.md),[^cite_note-518] and one in [Wylie Independent School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wylie_Independent_School_District_\(Collin_County,_Texas\)) in [St. Paul, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul,_Collin_County,_Texas), in the [Dallas-Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas-Fort_Worth) area.[^cite_note-519]

## Personal life

### Family

See also: [Bush family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_family)



Bush with his family in the [White House](33057.md) in 2007

Bush was engaged to Cathryn Lee Wolfman in 1967, but the engagement did not last. Bush and Wolfman remained on good terms after the end of the relationship.[^cite_note-520] While Bush was at a backyard barbecue in 1977, friends introduced him to [Laura Welch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Welch), a schoolteacher and librarian. After a three-month courtship, she accepted his marriage proposal and they wed on November 5 of that year.[^cite_note-readherlips-521] The couple settled in [Midland, Texas](151222.md). Bush left his family's [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)) to join his wife's [United Methodist Church](32304.md).[^cite_note-um-522] On November 25, 1981, Laura Bush gave birth to [fraternal twin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraternal_twin) daughters, [Barbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Bush_\(born_1981\)) and [Jenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenna_Bush_Hager).[^cite_note-readherlips-521] Bush describes being challenged by [Billy Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Graham) to consider faith in Jesus "Christ as the risen Lord", how he began reading the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) daily, "surrendering" to "the Almighty", that "faith is a walk" and that he was "moved by [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God)'s love".[^cite_note-Bush-2010-523]

### Alcohol abuse

Before his marriage, Bush repeatedly [abused alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_abuse).[^cite_note-Life-changing-524] On September 4, 1976, he was pulled over near his [family's summer home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker's_Point_Estate) in [Kennebunkport, Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennebunkport,_Maine), for [driving under the influence of alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driving_under_the_influence_of_alcohol). He was arrested for [DUI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driving_under_the_influence), was fined \$150, and received a brief suspension of his Maine driver's license.[^cite_note-525] Bush said that his wife has had a stabilizing effect on his life,[^cite_note-readherlips-521] and he attributes his decision to give up alcohol in 1986 to her influence.[^cite_note-turningpoint-526] While governor of Texas, Bush said of his wife, "I saw an elegant, beautiful woman who turned out not only to be elegant and beautiful, but very smart and willing to put up with my rough edges, and I must confess has smoothed them off over time."[^cite_note-readherlips-521] Bush also says that his faith in God was critical in abstaining: "I believe that God helped open my eyes, which were closing because of booze".[^cite_note-Bush-2010-523]

### Hobbies

Bush has been an avid reader throughout his adult life, preferring biographies and histories.[^cite_note-wp-cohen-527] During his presidency, Bush read the Bible daily,[^cite_note-bushmuseumofthebible-353] though at the end of his second term he said on television that he is "not a literalist" about Bible interpretation.[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529] [Walt Harrington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Harrington), a journalist, recalled seeing "books by [John Fowles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Fowles), [F. Scott Fitzgerald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._Scott_Fitzgerald), [James Joyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Joyce), and [Gore Vidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gore_Vidal) lying about, as well as biographies of [Willa Cather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willa_Cather) and [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria)" in his home when Bush was a Texas oilman. Other activities include cigar smoking and golf.[^cite_note-harrington2011-530] Bush has also painted many paintings. One of his best-known projects is a collection of 43 paintings of immigrants, titled *Out of Many, One*.[^cite_note-531] Another painting project was *Portraits of Courage: A Commander in Chief's Tribute to America's Warrior*.[^cite_note-532]

The discussion was moderated by James Glassmen, executive director of the George W. Bush Institute, which put out the book. President George W. Bush, who wrote the foreword to the book, made opening remarks. This book launch event was held at the Old Parkland Hospital in Dallas.
